<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/zus-health-secures-33m-build-infrastructure-ai-powered-clinical-workflows" hreflang="en">Zus Health secures $33M to build the infrastructure for AI-powered clinical workflows</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Zus Health secures $33M to build the infrastructure for AI-powered clinical workflows" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

As healthcare companies race to build AI copilots and agents, startup Zus Health is betting that the bigger opportunity is creating the connected patient data layer those applications need to work reliably.

Zus Health, Jonathan Bush's health tech company, picked up $33 million in series B funding to build out the data foundation for healthcare AI. Drive Capital led the series B round. Sanofi Ventures, HealthX Ventures and Alumni Ventures, as well as existing investors Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), F-Prime, Maverick Ventures and JAZZ Venture Partners also backed the round.

The financing brings Zus's total capital raised to more than $100 million, and the fresh funding will support the company's continued investment in its intelligent patient record platform, national healthcare data network and AI-powered clinical workflows.

Zus has built its platform in three layers, according to Bush. The company first focused on building a nationwide patient record, a data network that pulls information into that record, and, increasingly, AI-enabled workflows that automate care tasks using a more complete view of the patient.

Electronic health record systems (EHRs) succeeded at digitizing healthcare but failed to make patient information portable across organizations, Bush, founder and CEO of Zus Health, told Fierce Healthcare. Clinicians and care teams still spend valuable time searching for records and reconciling incomplete information. For patients, that can mean repeating their medical history at every appointment or relying on memory to recall past treatments. Advances in artificial intelligence now offer an opportunity to make healthcare information not just accessible, but actionable, according to Zus Health executives.

Launched in 2021, Zus Health developed what Bush calls an "always-on, FHIR-native, API-first medical record." It offers a shared health data platform designed to accelerate healthcare data interoperability by providing easy-to-use patient data at the point of care via standalone app, API (application programming interface), embedded components and direct EHR integration. At the center of its approach is an intelligent, continuously updated patient record that serves as a shared foundation for healthcare applications.

The company's Zus Aggregated Profile (ZAP) brings fragmented clinical information together into a unified, longitudinal patient record, supported by AI-powered clinical intelligence that makes the information easier to use across applications and workflows, according to the company. The platform also has nationwide connectivity across EHR networks, pharmacy data, care event notifications, labs and other clinical data sources that continuously updates the patient record without requiring customers to build their own connectivity infrastructure.

"We're finally on to the last and critical piece of what I think the future of medical records looks like, which is standing up workflows that automate all of the tasks of patient care in a very bespoke way," Bush told Fierce Healthcare. "[Zus Health] developed the first data-rich networked medical record platform in the industry's history. Whether Zeus wins or somebody else beats Zeus at it, all electronic medical records in the future will be national."

He added, "And now, with AI, we have an extraordinary opportunity to build workflows that actually make delivering care easier, faster and more effective."

The company is betting that the next competitive layer in healthcare AI will be the data infrastructure underneath the applications.

Zus Health, a Fierce 15 of 2025 honoree, wants to use its nationwide data network and longitudinal patient record as the infrastructure on which AI-powered clinical applications can run. The company's platform enables EHRs and other applications to work from a more complete, cross-provider view of the patient. Zus Health built AI solutions and flexible integration capabilities to put connected information to work across clinical and operational use cases, while enabling healthcare organizations to build applications tailored to their needs.

Zus works with value-based care organizations, digital health companies, provider organizations, as well as diagnostics and precision medicine companies. The company claims that in August alone its nationwide network supported more than 110 million networked transactions, while its AI-native clinical platform generated 39 million evidence citations year-to-date across its solutions—demonstrating both the scale of its connectivity and how patient information is being put to work across healthcare.

Zus supports solutions spanning pre-visit preparation, transitions of care, risk adjustment, quality gap closure, and population health management. Its clinical intelligence capabilities, including AI-powered generated patient summaries (GPS), turn fragmented patient records into information care teams can use more efficiently and confidently.

The company plans to use the series B funding to build out its AI-powered clinical intelligence and capabilities and broaden its healthcare data network connectivity. Zus plans to expand its solutions to automate population health and care management workflows, while enabling customers and developers to create AI-powered workflows across value-based care, specialty care and other high-value use cases.

The company is seeing rapid growth in specialty care, particularly oncology, where complex patient records are spread across multiple providers. Zeus’s continuously updated, aggregated patient data enables more comprehensive and efficient reviews of treatment eligibility, clinical trial criteria and genetic testing—tasks that traditionally required extensive manual work by nurses and analysts using fragmented legacy EHR systems, Bush noted.

"We believe healthcare is entering a new era in which AI can fundamentally change how care is delivered, but that transformation depends on having reliable, connected patient information," said Molly Bonakdarpour, general partner at Drive Capital, in a statement. "Zus has built the foundation to address that challenge at scale. Its combination of technology, market opportunity, and leadership gives us conviction in the company's ability to help shape the next generation of healthcare."

How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

CR80 card size in pixels, mm and inches: a developer's cheat sheet

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sooner or later someone asks you to build a membership card preview, a gift card mockup or a "download your staff ID" feature. The first question is always the same: how big is a card, really? The answer is more precise than "about 86 by 54", and getting it slightly wrong is how you end up with artwork that gets cropped at the printer.

This is the reference I wish I'd had: the numbers, the pixel maths, and copy-paste snippets.

A standard plastic card (bank card, loyalty card, ID badge) is the ID-1 format from ISO/IEC 7810. The printing industry calls it CR80.

Aspect ratio: 85.60 / 53.98 ≈ 1.586 : 1 (or 0.6306 the other way round). That's close to the golden ratio (1.618) but not equal to it, so don't reach for 1.618 as a shortcut.

You'll see 3.375 in quoted for the width in some printer drivers and US templates. That's 85.725 mm, a rounded imperial figure, not the ISO value. The difference is about 0.1 mm, which doesn't matter for a screen mockup, but use 85.60 mm when you're generating print files.

Printed cards are cut from larger sheets, and the cut can drift slightly. Two margins handle that:

With 3 mm bleed on every side, the artwork canvas is 91.60 × 59.98 mm (3.606 × 2.361 in). Always confirm which bleed the printer wants; 2 mm and 3 mm are both common.

Pixels = mm ÷ 25.4 × DPI. Rounded to whole pixels:

On screen, CSS treats 1 in as 96 px, so a "true size" card is about 323.5 × 204 CSS px. That's only physically accurate if the display and zoom happen to cooperate, which they usually don't (more on that below).

Note the rounding: 637.56 px rounds up to 638. If you build a 1011 × 637 canvas, you've quietly changed the aspect ratio. Keep the mm values as your source of truth and derive the pixels from them.

Percentage border-radius is calculated separately against width and height, so border-radius: 3.7% gives you slightly elliptical corners on a non-square box. Drive everything from one width variable instead:

Make .card position: relative if you use the safe-area box for layout. For a print stylesheet you can use real units:

Chromium honours @page size; check your target browser before relying on it.

SVG is the most honest format for this, because you can make one user unit equal 1 mm:

The negative viewBox origin puts (0, 0) on the trim corner, so all your layout coordinates are measured from the card edge, the same way a printer measures them. Remember to strip the guide rectangles before exporting the final file.

For a downloadable PNG, render at 300 DPI regardless of the user's screen:

For an on-screen preview, use ctx.roundRect(0, 0, W, H, 3.18) as a clip to show the rounded corners. Don't bake the rounding into a print file, though: the corners are die-cut, and the printer wants square artwork.

If you're generating card artwork in an app, it helps to drop the exported file over a template with the trim, bleed and safe lines drawn in. A free CR80 artwork checker does exactly that in the browser (files aren't uploaded), and if you just need a quick starting design to test your pipeline with, there's a free online card designer as well.

RESQ-X — An Earthquake Rescue Robot

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I am developing RESQ-X an engineering project designed to explore how robotics can help rescue teams inspect dangerous areas after earthquakes. The goal is to build a remotely operated robot that can explore uneven places provide information and help move small obstacles. Planned features include:

Raspberry Pi 5 as the main computer. Live video using a Raspberry Pi Camera Module. Remote control using a PlayStation 5 DualSense controller. Thermal sensing using an MLX90640 sensor to observe temperature differences. Servo powered lifting mechanism designed to move small rocks and lightweight debris. AI assisted driving to explore autonomous navigation. The project is being developed for the CCAAIBWS Project Olympiad. My goal is to develop and test the prototype see the results and if I win i will be on the next step more advanced that will be in London iWISE. This project allows me to explore robotics, electronics, programming and practical engineering while working on a real world challenge.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Market-cap weighting and company-specific risk

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It is good to wonder when one philandering CEO resigns today and one of the largest AI sector leading companies stock loses 100's of Billions in value in a day... dragging down Total market holding ETFs that are very heavily weighted in these tech companies.

Could it be portending of an AI induced bubble based on too much speculation?

May be a fair warning as to why not to blindly put the majority of your investments into a lopsided basket, i.e. Total market funds weighted with so much percentage of tech, perhaps.
A total market fund is always weighted toward the biggest companies and sectors. Isn't being lopsided in that way just the nature of the market? I'm not blind to, or uncomfortable with, that reality regarding my US equity allocation, and I wouldn't want to underweight tech given the world that we're living in. Company and sector leadership has changed more times than I can remember since I started investing over forty years ago, and I'm always happy owning the cap weighted market. Even as big names that have fallen out of favor, other companies have risen to replace them. Owning them all has worked out well for me.

How lagiga.info reads GigaBracket's results live

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 12 • Originally published at dimitriaigle.fr

lagiga.info, the GIGA'GAMES website, stores no tournament standings in its CMS. It reads them from a public GigaBracket route, embeds the bracket in an iframe and falls back to a static podium when the route doesn't answer. This article follows what moves from one product to the other, and in which direction.

GIGA'GAMES Federation Guadeloupe eSports runs two applications. GigaBracket, launched in June 2026, manages tournaments: the article on GigaBracket covers its formats and stack. lagiga.info is the public site: calendar, disciplines, news, directories and results.

The site splits its content across three sources, depending on who produces it:

Payload and PocketBase never intersect. Without either of them, the site still starts and serves its static content.

Standings depend on the tournament format, and only GigaBracket knows how to compute them. In elimination formats, they follow elimination depth rather than win count. lagiga recomputes nothing: it receives the result.

For this, GigaBracket exposes a route with no authentication, like its spectator views:

The shareId is the 12-character public identifier that /spectate links already use. The route answers with JSON whose key order stays stable, shortened here to a few fields:

An unknown shareId returns a 404 with not_found. A PocketBase outage returns a 503, an unexpected error a 500.

The full contract has 11 fields, and the results page displays only one: standings. The title, venue, date and player count come from Payload or the static fallback. The written report and the gallery exist only in Payload.

The join is the shareId field of the tournament-results Payload collection. A signed-in member fills it in from the admin. It feeds both the standings and the embedded bracket; when it is empty, neither appears.

The word "live" covers two different mechanisms on a results page:

During a running tournament, the bracket can therefore be one score ahead of the standings displayed just above it.

GigaBracket restricts where the embedding can happen. /embed/ routes declare lagiga's domains in frame-ancestors. Every other page answers frame-ancestors 'none' and X-Frame-Options: DENY.

fetchLiveResults never throws. It returns null if the shareId is empty, the response fails, the JSON is invalid or the network drops. The page then picks one of three displays:

Payload follows the same principle. If its database is missing or a migration hasn't run, the read fails cleanly and the site switches to the static fallback.

The results route isn't the only link. Both applications share the same PocketBase instance, and I recorded the rule in a project decision: the schema lives in GigaBracket, to avoid two sources of truth.

A second public route, /api/public/map-tournaments, feeds the /carte map with tournaments declared visible (mapVisible). The browser calls it when the visitor changes the filter, hence the open CORS headers.

This rule has a cost, also written in the decision: any new collection on the lagiga side goes through GigaBracket.

lagiga.info offers French, English and Spanish. Guadeloupean Creole stays served on /gcf/, outside the language switcher and the sitemap. Each layer covers a different scope:

The results route gets through this gap unharmed, because it returns data rather than sentences: names, ranks, numbers. Column labels come from lagiga's dictionary, so they follow the page language.

The iframe takes the hit. Its URL carries no language and GigaBracket picks its own: a browser asking for Spanish gets the bracket in French, inside a page in Spanish.

The #GCD, the federation's flagship event, brought together 7 tournaments and 143 participants on July 25 and 26, 2026 in Sainte-Anne. Those tournaments ran on start.gg and Challonge, and six later joined GigaBracket through import scripts: four from start.gg, two from Challonge. For this edition, the route therefore serves closed results.

The seventh, Domino Clash, was played on site. Its shareId is null: its page never calls GigaBracket and shows 13 participants and a static podium of three names.

Our CI Failed 40% of the Time and the Code Was Fine: A Flaky Test

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Just re-run it." Four words that quietly destroyed the trust of an entire engineering team.

If you've ever typed those words into a Slack thread, this story is for you.

It was 4:47 PM on a Thursday. Priya, a backend engineer at a 12-person B2B SaaS startup, pushed a one-line fix for a typo in an email template. The pipeline went red.

She didn't read the logs. She didn't even open the failed job. She clicked Re-run all jobs, grabbed a coffee, and came back to a green build.

Nothing in the code had changed. The build had simply decided to pass the second time.

That moment, repeated dozens of times a week, is what we'll dissect in this case study. By the end of this post you will have:

The team's product was a subscription management dashboard. Their stack looked like most modern startups:

On paper, this was a healthy setup. In practice, the end-to-end suite had become the thing everybody worked around instead of relying on.

The team's engineering manager finally did what every engineering manager should do before complaining: she measured.

She pulled 30 days of CI history and wrote a small script to classify every failed run by whether a re-run of the same commit later passed.

Eighty-one percent of the failures were not bugs. They were noise.

She then did the math that got leadership's attention:

That is nearly half of one engineer's monthly capacity, burned on re-running tests that were not even telling the truth. And the invisible cost was worse: engineers had started ignoring red builds. Eventually, a real bug slipped through because everyone assumed "it's probably flaky again."

Before looking for a new approach, the team tried the usual suspects.

Attempt 1: Add retries. They set retries: 2 in the Playwright config.

Result: the pipeline went green more often, but run time increased by 35%, and the flakiness was now hidden rather than fixed.

Attempt 2: Add hard waits. Somebody sprinkled await page.waitForTimeout(3000) everywhere.

Result: slower tests, same flakiness, and a codebase full of magic numbers.

Attempt 3: A "flaky test quarantine." They tagged unstable tests and skipped them in CI.

Result: the quarantine list grew from 9 tests to 31 in six weeks. A quarantine that only grows is just a graveyard.

The team finally sorted the 139 flaky failures by cause. This is the table that changed their thinking:

Nearly half of the pain came from tests that were coupled to implementation details of the UI. A selector like this one was typical:

A developer changes a wrapper div, nothing visible changes for the user, and the test dies.

The insight was simple but uncomfortable: the tests were asserting on how the page was built, not on what a user could do.

The workflow they adopted looked like this:

A scenario that used to be 40 lines of Playwright became something like this:

No nth-child. No waitForTimeout. The test reads like the requirement it protects.

They did not rewrite everything at once. Here is the order that worked, and it's a good template for you:

Wire the new suite into the pipeline like any other quality gate:

Illustrative numbers from the scenario, tracked using the same scripts from earlier:

The most important number is "Failures that were real bugs." When a build turns red now, people read the logs. That is what a healthy signal looks like.

Run the flaky-report.sh script against your own repository this week. If more than 15% of your failures disappear on re-run, you don't have a testing problem, you have a trust problem.

Have a flaky-test horror story of your own? Drop it in the comments. I read every one.

Lokutor Is Now a LiveKit Agents Plugin

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 12 • Edited on Nov 12 • Originally published at lokutor.com

Lokutor is now a plugin for LiveKit Agents. livekit-plugins-lokutor was merged into livekit/agents on 12 November 2026, and it gives any LiveKit agent our speech recognition and our voices.

LiveKit is open-source infrastructure for realtime audio and video. LiveKit Agents is its Python framework for voice agents that join a room or a phone call. The framework runs the conversation: the room, turn-taking, interruptions and the language model. Plugins supply the speech in and out.

Both work in English, Spanish, Catalan, Galician, Basque, Portuguese, French, Italian and German. Each direction is one model for all nine languages, so there is no per-language routing to set up.

If you already run a LiveKit agent, switching to Lokutor's speech means changing the stt= and tts= lines; the rest of the agent stays as it is.

The package is expected to be published to PyPI with LiveKit's next release. Until then, the integration guide shows how to install it from LiveKit's repository.

If you want Lokutor to run the whole conversation, including the LLM, turn-taking, tools, phone numbers and recordings, use a Lokutor agent instead. The plugin is for teams who already build on LiveKit and want our speech inside their pipeline.

Thanks to the LiveKit team, who reviewed the plugin closely over several rounds. If you build something with it, tell us at contact@lokutor.com.

Try the voices in your browser on Hugging Face. Lokutor is live on Product Hunt.

Procter & Gamble vs. Starbucks: Which Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Procter & Gamble's steady cash machine goes head-to-head with Starbucks' bid for a brand-led comeback, and the valuation gap may tip the scales.

Choosing between Procter & Gamble (PG +0.21%) and Starbucks (SBUX -2.39%) requires balancing the steady reliability of essential household goods against the growth potential of a global coffee recovery story.

Procter & Gamble serves billions with daily necessities, while Starbucks relies on brand loyalty in the premium beverage market. Both companies face distinct macroeconomic shifts as they aim to deliver value heading into 2027.

Procter & Gamble operates as a global leader in consumer packaged goods, selling trusted brands across categories like beauty, grooming, and home care. The company reaches customers through diverse channels, including grocery stores and digital commerce. A significant portion of its sales comes from mass merchandisers, and Walmart (WMT +0.41%) accounts for nearly 16% of total net sales. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, especially since the top ten customers represent roughly 43% of its sales.

Financial performance remained steady in FY 2026, with revenue reaching approximately $87.0 billion, a 3.3% increase over the previous year. The company reported net income of close to $16.0 billion, maintaining a strong net margin of 18.4%. This net margin represents the percentage of revenue that remains as profit after all expenses are paid. This consistent profitability underscores the company's ability to manage costs while navigating a competitive global marketplace for essential goods.

As of its June 2026 balance sheet, the company reported a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.6x. This ratio measures total debt relative to shareholder equity, indicating that the company uses a moderate amount of borrowing to finance its operations. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term obligations with short-term assets, was roughly 0.7x. Furthermore, the company generated free cash flow of nearly $15.1 billion. Free cash flow is the cash a company generates after accounting for the money spent to maintain or expand its physical assets.

Starbucks continues to expand its global footprint with more than 41,000 stores across 86 markets. The company is currently implementing its "Back to Starbucks" strategy to revitalize store experiences and improve operational efficiency. It relies heavily on its Global Coffee Alliance with Nestlé (NSRGF +0.03%) to distribute packaged products. In its latest annual report, the company described its focus on being one of the top retail stocks globally by leaning into its North American segment, which accounts for roughly 74% of revenue.

In FY 2026, the coffee giant reported revenue of approximately $37.9 billion, representing growth of 2% compared to the prior year. Net income for the period was nearly $2.5 billion, resulting in a net margin of close to 6.7%. While revenue has grown, the company is working to improve its bottom-line efficiency. This focus on profitability is central to its long-term plan to navigate changing consumer habits and rising costs in its global store network.

Regarding its financial health, the balance sheet dated September 2026 showed a debt-to-equity ratio of -2.9x. This negative value means that total liabilities exceed shareholder equity. The current ratio stands at approximately 0.8x, suggesting a tight but manageable position for meeting short-term debts. During the same period, Starbucks generated free cash flow of nearly $3.7 billion. This cash generation is critical for funding its store renovations and returning capital to shareholders through dividends and buybacks.

Procter & Gamble faces risks from geopolitical and macroeconomic volatility, which can disrupt global supply chains and inflate material costs. Fluctuations in foreign currencies and trade policy changes may also impact its international profitability. The company must navigate an intense retail landscape where competition for shelf space remains high. Additionally, its dependence on complex IT systems exposes it to potential cybersecurity threats and data breaches that could disrupt its global operations.

Starbucks is subject to risks regarding its brand relevance and the successful execution of its new strategic initiatives. The company relies on high-quality arabica coffee, making it vulnerable to commodity price volatility and climate-related disruptions in coffee-growing regions. Labor relations and potential unionization also present challenges, as they may lead to increased wage inflation and higher operational costs. Furthermore, the company faces legal and financial uncertainty from evolving global tax rules and potential litigation in various markets.

Procter & Gamble offers a lower earnings multiple, while Starbucks appears more attractively priced when comparing its market value to its sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Starbucks. It's committed to a major turnaround, and the results are already showing up in its profits. After sales slumped, new CEO Brian Niccol launched a plan called "Back to Starbucks" in 2024 to win customers back with faster service, simpler menus, and refreshed coffeehouses. Comparable sales have now grown for five straight quarters, and adjusted earnings per share jumped 54% last quarter. With more than 1,000 new coffeehouses planned worldwide, the recovery is turning into expansion.

In its recently reported fiscal Q4 2026, Starbucks beat earnings estimates, and its margins widened for the third straight quarter. Even with shoppers watching their budgets, more U.S. customers came through the door, and each one spent more.

To give P&G its due, it owns household names like Tide, Pampers, and Gillette, and shoppers buy its everyday essentials in good times and bad. But as a mature business, it grows slowly, and higher costs can squeeze its profits. Starbucks, by contrast, is growing again, with a successful turnaround in place and a fiscal 2028 plan ahead of schedule. That makes it my pick for the long haul.

Ellen Pompeo Mostly Loves Grey's Fans, But I'd Be Annoyed By One Thing They Do As Well

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One Thing Drew Barrymore Does For Her Talk Show Kelly Clarkson Didn't: 'It Works!'

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Grey’s Anatomy is currently in its 23rd season on the 2026 TV schedule, which is still hard to comprehend. Given that the fans are still tuning in, though, no matter what that hospital has been through, it makes sense. While Ellen Pompeo loves Grey’s Anatomy fans, who have been keeping the show on the air for over 20 years, there is just one thing that annoys her. And I can’t say I blame her.

Pompeo starred in Grey’s Anatomy as the titular Dr. Meredith Grey since the very beginning, before ultimately departing as a series regular midway through Season 19. She’s remained on as narrator and executive producer, frequently returning to the halls of Grey Sloan Memorial in a guest capacity. Having been a staple on television for over 20 years, you’d imagine that Pompeo runs into a lot of fans. Unfortunately, it’s not always the greatest experience. The actress told Magic Radio that even though she loves the fans, she’s annoyed by one thing that would honestly annoy me, too:

To be honest, I’m so grateful for the show and so grateful for the fans, and I love them. But to be honest, when people call me Meredith, I do get a little bit annoyed. So I do get a little annoyed with that. I love that you love the show, but just call me by my right name. And everybody’s so nice, and they just say, you know, ‘I love you so much.’ And so, how really can I be upset at that?

I get why people would call celebrities by their characters’ names. You see them as their characters on television or in movies, and in the moment, you spot them and call them by their signature role. That being said, fans should probably be saying the celebrity’s name rather than their character’s, and I am someone who is a stickler for that. I would definitely find that annoying, because you’re meeting the actual person, not the fictional character. They’re two different people.

For a show like Grey’s Anatomy that has been on for over two decades, I can get why it’s especially annoying as people have had 20 years to Meredith Grey’s actual name. That being said, Pompeo doesn’t seem to have any ill will towards the loyal Grey’s Anatomy fans. Sure, she’s annoyed when they don’t call her by her name, but she still loves them anyway. Folks may just want to be a bit more aware of what they say when they meet Pompeo or any other celebrity and make sure you’re getting the name right.

The actress is not on Grey’s Anatomy as much anymore, but whenever she does return to the medical drama, you never know what to expect. It’s always hard to see a Grey’s Anatomy star leave the series, but in Pompeo’s case, she’s simply not a series regular anymore, but is still a recurring actor. Of course, you can’t have Grey’s Anatomy without the Dr. Meredith Grey, so it’s not like the show could wipe the slate completely clean.

As of now, it’s hard to tell how long Grey’s Anatomy will remain on the air, but there doesn’t seem to be any end in sight. Meaning fans will have plenty of time to practice their hellos to Pompeo if they ever meet her so that they can get the name right. If anything, there are 23 seasons of Grey’s Anatomy reminding you who is starring in the series, so that’s a great place to start, and they’re all streaming with a Hulu subscription.

Colette Kress Has Been Named Interim Chief of Nvidia. But There’s Another Executive Favored to Replace Jensen Huang

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Kress is the logical pick for now, as the company's longtime CFO. But other executives, both inside and outside the company, are being discussed.

Although Jensen Huang has officially resigned as CEO of Nvidia (NVDA -4.34%), a new story is quickly emerging: who will replace the man many call the Godfather of artificial intelligence.

AI is a generational, game-changing new technology, and Huang founded Nvidia over three decades ago, so whoever replaces Huang has enormous shoes to fill.

Nvidia's CFO Colette Kress has been named interim CEO. It's a logical choice. Kress has been CFO of Nvidia for 13 years, is always on conference calls, and knows Nvidia's numbers likely better than anyone.

However, another executive is favored to replace Huang in the top job as of this writing.

As of 5:53 p.m. ET on Nov. 12, Kalshi assigned a 42% chance that a Nvidia executive named Ian Buck would become Nvidia’s next CEO. Kress has the next-highest chance at 13%, and there are others in the running as well.

Buck, according to his LinkedIn profile, has been a vice president and general manager of Nvidia for nearly 14 years and has worked at Nvidia for over 22 years. Buck completed his undergraduate studies at Princeton University and then earned a Ph.D. in computer science from Stanford.

According to various biographies, Buck was the development lead for Brook, the programming language that enabled developers to leverage the parallel processing capabilities of graphics processing units (GPUs) for general-purpose computing. Buck is also credited with creating CUDA, which stands for Compute Unified Device Architecture.

CUDA came out in 2006 and has served as a key moat for Nvidia and its GPUs. CUDA is a parallel computing platform and programming model that helps developers build applications with Nvidia's GPUs. CUDA has become the industry standard for deploying new AI applications, providing Nvidia with a strong moat.

Investors can think of CUDA as an operating system that runs only on Nvidia chips. So even though there are competitors that can build chips just as powerful as Nvidia, developers will be less prone to switch because they would have to use a different computing platform that they may not know as well.

The Huang scandal and quick departure stunned much of Wall Street, which is why the stock has tanked over the past week. Many analysts and pundits also speculated that there was no succession plan in place prior to the scandal.

Buck could make sense for several reasons. Even though Huang is tough to replace, Nvidia still has a great product in high demand. One question, though, is what does innovation look like after Huang.

After all, Nvidia frequently releases new chip models. Buck provides investors with reassurance that the product is unlikely to go stale. His hiring would resemble Apple's recent decision to name the longtime Apple executive and former senior vice president of hardware engineering, John Ternus, as its new CEO to replace Tim Cook.

"He (Buck) could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," Charu Chanana, an investment strategist at the investment bank Saxo, said in a research note, according to the BBC.

With Huang gone, there are still many concerns about Nvidia, particularly regarding the company's relationship with China and its sales to foreign companies that may have illegally re-exported the chips to China.

Chanana raises an interesting point, as one of Huang's big tasks over the past year-and-a-half has been convincing the Trump administration to allow Nvidia to sell chips in China. Buck may not have experience in this realm, which will likely face even more scrutiny moving forward.

While Kalshi offers some insight, the prediction markets platform has certainly been wrong many times before, and we don't know how long it will take Nvidia to name a new CEO -- it could be tomorrow or months from now.

There are also other candidates who have a chance of becoming Nvidia's CEO, according to Kalshi, including former Nvidia executive Rene Haas, who is now the CEO of Arm Holdings, and Apple's Chief Hardware Officer Johny Srouji.

But right now, Buck is the clear favorite.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Abbiamo ritrovato l'Italia. Quella che sa mettere in campo cuore, qualità, coraggio. Non ero favorevole al ritorno sulla panchina azzurra di Roberto Mancini dopo la fuga per vil denaro tra le braccia degli sceicchi. Ma in pochi mesi il nuovo-vecchio cittì ci ha restituito una Nazionale di cui essere orgogliosi. E' vero, a San Siro non c'era la vera Francia. Ma gli azzurri hanno prima sofferto poi voluto e meritato una vittoria che ci lascia in corsa per la vittoria nel girone di Nations League. Sarà decisiva la sfida di domenica a Bruxelles contro il Belgio. La presuntuosa Francia non è di un altro pianeta.

C'é tanta Inter in questa Nazionale che ci ha fatto innamorare. La doppietta di Pio Esposito proietta questo talento a livello internazionale. Il bomber nerazzurro ha ancora grandi margini di crescita. Ma ha personalità e fiuto del gol. Ne ha segnati 9 in 14 presenze in azzurro. Numeri da favola. Frattesi è pronto a compiere l'ultimo salto di qualità.

Altri protagonisti azzurri. Cambiaghi ha fatto impazzire la difesa della Francia. Finalmente un esterno d'attacco puro. Micidiale nell'uno contro uno. Capace di puntare la porta ma anche di consegnare assist ai compagni. L'estate scorsa il Bologna ha rifiutato offerte da trenta milioni per Cambiaghi da club italiani (Como) e stranieri (Betis). Tra pochi mesi questo talento diventerà uno dei protagonisti del mercato. Ha triplicato il suo valore anche il giovane Kayode. Liquidato dalla Fiorentina per non fare ombra a Dodò. Oggi i 18 milioni versati dal Brentford sono diventati una cinquantina. La Fiorentina avrebbe bisogno assoluto di uno come Kayode. Certe scelte sono dei clamorosi autogol.

Con questa Italia ora torniamo a sognare. Ed è bello che la grande impresa degli azzurri sia avvenuta davanti agli occhi di un fuoriclasse come Antonelli fresco di titolo mondiale. Lo sport italiano ha voglia di tornare a essere protagonista ovunque. Anche nel calcio.

Bottomline Completes First USDC Transfers Inside Its Enterprise Payments Platform

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Bottomline has completed live USDC transfers inside its enterprise payments platform. The initial transfer was between company entities.

Bottomline disclosed the cross-border transactions in a November 10 announcement, using its Global Payments Hub and Cash Management platform.

The practical point is where the payment fits. A finance team may be more interested in approving, tracking and reconciling a transfer within its existing systems than in opening a separate crypto application to make it happen.

Bottomline describes the milestone as an intra-company transfer. That establishes that the company used the integration for a live movement of funds; it does not establish that unrelated businesses are already using it at scale.

The release says the aim is to let treasury teams handle stablecoin payments alongside traditional methods, retaining familiar approvals, controls and audit processes. It presents near-real-time settlement for selected scenarios, especially cross-border payments, as part of the attraction.

The company has not supplied a transaction-by-transaction performance dataset in this announcement. Readers therefore cannot use it to calculate an average fee saving or a reliable improvement in end-to-end delivery time.

Bottomline’s cash-management product description emphasises connecting treasury, accounting, compliance, banking and enterprise resource planning data. It describes tools for cash visibility, reconciliation and reporting, helping place the USDC announcement within the wider product.

Consider a group with businesses in two countries. Moving value between them is only one part of the job. Staff may also need to identify the correct entities, record the purpose of the transfer, obtain approval and match the completed payment to the accounts.

If a stablecoin route requires those steps to be reconstructed manually outside the normal workflow, a fast blockchain transfer can still leave an expensive administrative task. Integration is useful when it keeps the financial record attached to the movement of money.

NewsBTC explored a related implementation issue in Circle and ACI’s Latin American settlement collaboration, which examined how a digital-dollar option fits within existing payment operations.

Circle describes USDC as a dollar-redeemable token backed by liquid reserves. That explains the asset being transferred, but a corporate payment can involve more than the onchain movement itself.

A business funding a transfer from a bank account needs an entry route. A recipient that pays suppliers in local currency may need conversion and a bank payout. Each stage can affect the amount received and the time before it can be spent.

For that reason, the useful comparison is between two complete payment routes serving the same need. A low network charge does not settle the comparison if conversion costs are higher, or if an exception requires substantial staff time to resolve.

The next useful disclosures would identify supported use cases, the businesses choosing them and performance over repeated transfers. Clear handling of failures, rejected payments and reconciliation exceptions would be particularly informative.

There is also a difference between a first transaction and a mature operating metric, as NewsBTC’s analysis of Bitso Business payment volumes explains. Neither measure should be mistaken for fee revenue or universal customer availability.

Bottomline has demonstrated a concrete integration step. Its commercial value will come from making a recurring treasury task easier to complete and account for, with enough transparency for finance teams to compare the new route with the options they already trust.

Shop of Horrors: How Talent Representation Firm Verve Became Hollywood’s Home for Successful Scares

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When the box office grosses started rolling in this summer from Curry Barker’s “Obsession” and Kane Parsons’ “Backrooms,” social media wags predicted what Hollywood would do next: raid every Reddit thread and low-budget YouTube channel for the next big digital creator turned auteur.

In rapid succession, the major studio players greenlit splashy genre projects based on internet lore and the fables that kept Gen Z awake at night as kids. The leadership at Verve, a talent and literary firm that has given its three-letter rivals a run for their money for almost 20 years, couldn’t help laughing. Related Stories 'KPop Demon Hunters,' 'Squid Game' Stars Fete Netflix's Korea Decade as Ted Sarandos Teases 'Tantara,' 'All of Us Are Dead' Season 2 'Nobody Wants This' Renewed for Season 4 at Netflix

“We joke about it sometimes, how novel it became to activate fan bases and appreciate good, artistic horror this year,” says David Boxerbaum, a partner and motion picture lit agent at Verve. “But for us, it’s always been cool. It’s always been an area of specialization and really intense focus for our business.”

They’re dead serious. Co-founders Adam Levine and Bryan Besser, along with agent Michael Bitar (formerly head of development at Sony’s Screen Gems), have quietly stacked a deep roster of spooky superstars — negotiating dozens of deals and media rights sales in 2026 alone. The founders disclosethat transactions around horror for writing, producing, directing and acting clients have helped growrevenue nearly 20% for a second consecutive year.

Most prominent was the heated auction for the film and TV adaptive licenses to the “Texas Chainsaw Massacre” franchise. Representing the rights holders, Verve fielded offers from 101 Studios, Amazon MGM, Blumhouse, Neon, Netflix, Paramount, Sony and Universal. Indie studio A24 defeated them all, attaching “Obsession” helmer Barker to direct a film and Glen Powell to produce a TV series inspired by the “Chainsaw” serial killer Leatherface.

Clients Zach Lipovsky and Adam B. Stein shot to fame in 2025 for co-directing “Final Destination: Bloodlines” — a revival that cost $50 million for New Line and netted almost $320 million at the worldwide box office. Verve has set up 12 development projects for Lipovsky and Stein across town (including “Gremlins 3” at Warner Bros. and “Metal Gear Solid” at Sony, where the pair now enjoy a first-look deal courtesy of their agents). Canadian illustrator and world-builder Trevor Henderson, a Verve client, was one of the first creatives to benefit from the “Backrooms” boom. That was in July, when Henderson’s macabre digital-first story “Siren Head” sold to Warner Bros. as a feature film to be co-written by “Weapons” mastermind Zach Cregger.

Meanwhile, Verve talent agent Josh Rahm rebooted the career of “Scream” favorite Matthew Lillard, carving a new path for the star via the “Five Nights at Freddy’s” franchise and Amazon’s hit series reboot of “Carrie” from Mike Flanagan. Boxerbaum also helped producer Scott Glassgold establish 12:01 Films, a company dedicated to creator content; 12:01 has been a leader in the booming market for short stories adaptable to film and TV. Glassgold even has a specialty imprint at Simon & Schuster, adding publishing to 12:01’s robust pipeline.

The secret, top Verve brass say, is embracing their adoration for horror (and other genres too; the agency has a similar foothold in the animation space). It’s also having the foresight to know that their multi-hyphenate clients aren’t just genre stars — they’re versatile storytellers who can take on any quadrant they please.

“An ongoing theme for years in the horror business is that these projects are concept driven, not star driven,” says Bitar. “These filmmakers are becoming the stars more so than the faces in front of the camera.” Verve’s agents devote just as much energy to growing with their talent, lest they lose them to the corporate agencies promising crossover abundance. Disloyalty, after all, is an agent’s true horror story.

Build once, run on any agent: A practical guide to agent skills

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

How open source is driving positive change.

Organizations worldwide are incorporating open source methodologies into the way they build and ship their own software.

Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

Agent skills capture repeatable engineering work in files teams can review and reuse, while tools, project context, and permissions remain specific to the host that runs them.

How many times this week did you re-explain the same task to an agent? How many times did you ask your colleague with deep institutional knowledge how to solve a specific problem? That context shouldn’t be lost in a chat or locked in one person’s head. Agent skills let you capture repeatable instructions so you and your agents can reuse them. The skill you write for yourself tonight can later become part of a team’s pipeline.

An agent skill is a versioned directory centered on a SKILL.md file that describes when and how to do a repeatable task. You can share those instructions across agents, but the environment still needs to provide the tools, credentials, and permissions to carry them out. A skill can describe how to query a database, for example, but it doesn’t grant access to that database.

I covered this topic live with Shishir Tewari, Senior Manager, Data Engineering at Procore, during GitHub Universe 2026. Our session was titled “Build once, run on any agent: a practical guide to agent skills”.

A skill works best when it has a clear job, input, and output. For example, let’s say you’re building an agent that checks AI-generated changes against the original request. If the same agent assesses its own work, its explanation might influence the review. With Proof Agent, we use a separate verifier to examine the changes and provide evidence for its findings. The skill’s frontmatter names the task and describes its purpose:

At the center is a simple rule: self-verification is not verification.

The When to Verify section in Proof Agent’s SKILL.md says when to invoke the skill. The remaining instructions explain what the verifier reviews and what evidence to include in its findings.

When the judgment that makes a data pipeline correct lives in four different people’s heads, those four people are the roadmap. Each change to the pipeline depends on their availability and knowledge of the data. Data Forge is an example of how to capture parts of that judgment in skills that work together.

In the support-desk pipeline example, we start with a request for metrics that count incidents created and resolved. Before building the pipeline, the forge-init skill discovers details such as source tables and naming conventions, then writes them to a resolved manifest. The other skills read that manifest for the project context they need.

The forge-builder skill then coordinates the work from the issue through to a pull request. It uses forge-dbx to query the data platform and forge-lint to apply the team’s standards as it designs and builds the pipeline. A person reviews the proposed decisions along the way, and the resulting pull request contains the pipeline code, schema changes, and tests.

Each skill has a defined responsibility, and they share project context instead of rediscovering it independently. When a step changes, you can change one skill and leave the rest alone.

Skills fit bounded, repeatable work that benefits from reusable judgment, such as a checklist, release, migration, or metric pipeline. If a workflow needs a stable interface, shared state, an audit trail, long-running control, or permissions enforced outside the model, an application is a better fit.

We don’t want to hardcode every project’s names, database, or conventions into the skills. In the Data Forge support-desk example, a manifest describes the project, metric store, and platform so the skills can use the right project details without baking them into their instructions:

With the discovered details in the resolved manifest, the other skills don’t have to make their own assumptions about the project.

Keep discovered project details separate from team standards. The forge-init initialization skill can inspect existing schemas and code patterns; the forge-lint skill supplies the rules to follow before code is generated. A pattern found in a repository isn’t automatically a rule to preserve, so make discovered details reviewable and let people confirm or change them where business judgment is needed. To check portability, ask: could another team use these skills with its own manifest without rewriting them?

The forge-dbx skill gives the other Data Forge skills one way to access the data platform. It reads the manifest and selects a Databricks or DuckDB adapter, so each skill doesn’t need its own connection and query logic.

The manifest can choose DuckDB for the local support-desk demo, but it can’t install the DuckDB CLI or grant database access. Those remain setup responsibilities for the environment and its user. The manifest picks the engine; the host provides the access.

The Data Forge support-desk workflow has seven phases, with a human checkpoint at each one. The workflow overview shows how the skills fit together. Before a phase proceeds, the workflow checks that the previous phase is complete; before schema changes run, it asks for approval.

The Proof Agent example applies the same principle to code review. A GitHub Actions workflow gives the verifier the original request and code changes, but not the coding agent’s own assessment. The verifier returns PASS, FAIL, or PARTIAL and must cite evidence in the changed files. The GitHub Action does a static review of the diff; it doesn’t execute code or run tests. In the demo workflow, we set block-on-fail: false so findings were advisory. By default, a FAIL fails the check, which blocks the merge if the check is required. The aim is to keep test results and merge requirements in your repository’s CI checks and rules.

Supporting multiple clients can mean keeping several copies of the same instructions in sync. In the Data Forge example, we keep skills/*/SKILL.md as the source and use a packaging script to generate client-specific files: .claude-plugin/plugin.json for Claude Code, .cursor-plugin/plugin.json for Cursor, and .github/agents/*.agent.md for GitHub Copilot. The script also includes a check mode for CI:

Clients can differ in their available models, tools, credentials, and permissions. After installing a skill, check that the client recognizes it for the intended task, loads its references, can use the required tools, and returns the evidence the skill calls for.

Start with the task you’re tired of explaining. Write down what it needs, where it stops, and what a reviewer sees. Then try it with another agent and improve it as you learn what works.

CNCF data shows cloud native is the on-ramp to AI, and maintainers are paying the price

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The global population of cloud-native developers has topped 21 million, but the headcount tells only part of the story. Artificial intelligence is changing what those developers build and how open-source projects absorb their work.

Fresh research is putting numbers on that shift. The latest State of Cloud Native Development report from the Cloud Native Computing Foundation and SlashData Ltd. counts more than 21 million practitioners, or 41% of developers worldwide. Yet that figure likely misses many people who rely on cloud-native infrastructure without knowing it, according to Bob Killen (pictured, right), director of technical program management at CNCF.

“The big thing is it actually might be undercounting a significant amount of them,” Killen said. “Because there’s a very large amount of people that are essentially consuming cloud-native applications without even realizing it.”

Killen and Liam Bollmann-Dodd (left), principal market research consultant at SlashData, spoke with theCUBE’s Rebecca Knight at KubeCon + CloudNativeCon NA, during an exclusive broadcast on theCUBE, SiliconANGLE Media’s livestreaming studio. They discussed the latest State of Cloud Native Development findings, AI adoption, the strain on open-source maintainers and the shift toward cost discipline. (* Disclosure below.)

The report’s most striking finding concerns AI. About 75% of cloud-native developers use AI in some form, compared with only about 55% of other developers, Bollmann-Dodd noted. The gap mirrors a broader trend of agentic AI pushing Kubernetes into a second act.

“It’s a massive difference,” Bollmann-Dodd said. “So somehow being cloud native enables you and your organization and your personal stack to do AI in all its forms.”

The same AI wave is straining the people who keep the ecosystem running. Sustainability dominated the Maintainer Summit ahead of KubeCon, as maintainers face a flood of pull requests frequently generated by agents, Killen explained.

“How do we as maintainers keep up with the flooding of PRs and issues and things like that?” Killen said. “Especially when many of them are frankly just slop. And the real frustrating part is they’ll be just good enough.”

Cost discipline is the next shift. Just as financial operations practices followed rising cloud bills, cloud-native developers will soon manage model and token spending closely, according to Bollmann-Dodd.

“I think token maxing is dead and it’s going to stay dead forever,” Bollmann-Dodd said. “And I think it’s going to become much more focused on how you spend your money and how you spend your tokens.”

Here’s the complete video interview, part of SiliconANGLE’s and theCUBE’s coverage of KubeCon + CloudNativeCon NA:

(* Disclosure: The Linux Foundation sponsored this segment of theCUBE. Neither The Linux Foundation nor other sponsors have editorial control over content on theCUBE or SiliconANGLE.)

Our new proprietary theCUBE AI Video Cloud is breaking ground in audience interaction, leveraging theCUBEai.com neural network to help technology companies make data-driven decisions and stay at the forefront of industry conversations.
