The upcoming Fable reboot is set to launch on February 23, 2027, and with that date growing inexorably closer Xbox has dropped a new gameplay trailer showcasing four minutes of heroic questing across a rather grim-looking slice of Albion.

The trailer shows off a little bit of everything: some cinematics, some chitchat, the UI, and of course plenty of combat: melee, ranged, and even a bit of magic.

Today we're sharing four minutes of new @fable gameplay. It's been a long road to bring this franchise back, and I'm proud of the work Playground Games is doing. Take a look. https://t.co/q74VcEt5xENovember 12, 2026

"The Hero of Briar Hill has entered the depths of Deepwood alongside Arden, a local Hero, to investigate why strange magical happenings have been troubling the people of Oakshire. Here, they cross a threshold where the natural magic of Deepwood appears corrupted by an outside force, and they discover hints as to who or what could be responsible..."

The reaction to the video is quite positive among the YouTube comments but somewhat more subdued on the PC Gaming subreddit, where more than one poster suggested that it doesn't really show the game in its best light—which is to say that it doesn't really look like Fable, or at least what Fable fans expected. Fable 3 may not be everyone's favorite game in the series but it was bright, colorful, exuberant—this video, by comparison, seems downright grim and conventional.

That's about where I land on it: A four-minute clip obviously isn't going to tell the whole story, but with the video being showcased as part of Xbox's 25th anniversary, and the full Fable release now just three months away (unless it's delayed again), it's reasonable to assume that Playground and Xbox would want to put their best foot forward. And, well, this is fine, but I can't say it has me all charged up to play Fable: To me, it just feels kind of flat.

But maybe that's just me—we'll find out in February. Fable on the PC is available for pre-purchase now on Steam.

Democrats launch Nvidia investigation, write scathing letter on Huang’s ‘scheme’ with Trump

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Democrats on Thursday opened an inquiry into how the Trump administration handled Nvidia’s chip sales to China. Earlier on Thursday, the company ousted its CEO Jensen Huang. He also agreed to testify to a different set of Republican-led Congressional committees.

Huang was removed from the company he founded after he confessed to an extramarital affair amid reports of a relationship with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy. (He has since been accused of having more affairs, with Nvidia employees.)

The alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, was an executive of Nvidia customer Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company that U.S. officials suspect of helping route advanced Nvidia chips into China. The Democratic inquiry comes from Rep. Richard Neal (D-Mass.), the top Democrat on the House Ways and Means Committee, and Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.), the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee. As the minority party, they can ask questions but can’t compel answers.

Their humdinger of a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick asks the department to preserve records about an arrangement they find questionable and, they allege, illegal.

“The scheme worked like this,” as the letter describes it. President Trump agreed to sales of Nvidia’s H200 chips to China as long as the chips, manufactured by Taiwan, were first shipped to the United States. On arriving, Trump added a tariff “effectively taking a cut of Nvidia’s sales to China through what appears to be an unconstitutional export tax system,” the letter said. Then the Commerce Department, under Lutnick, issued an export license.

By the way, the deal between Trump and Nvidia to share revenue was public. In August 2025, Nvidia and AMD both agreed to share 15% of their revenue from chip sales to China with the U.S. government as part of a deal to secure export licenses, the AP reported at the time.

But the letter questions why Lutnick continued to allow Megaspeed to buy chips even after he was reportedly informed by US intelligence of their suspicions of Alice Huang and of Jensen Huang’s relationship with her. It questionswhy the department reportedly green-lit other Asian companies to buy Nvidia chips “with no physical presence at their listed addresses.” So the inquiry is aimed at the administration, not just Nvidia and Huang.

The letter also questions how Nvidia’s donations to Trump’s projects factored in. It notes that the export deal was approved after Trump learned of “Mr. Huang’s compromised position” in sleeping with an alleged spy, and after Nvidia donated $26 million toward Trump’s projects, including the ballroom. It appears to be asking if Trump used his knowledge of the affair to secure donations from Nvidia.

Interestingly, in a “60 Minutes” interview with President Trump that aired on Sunday, he mentioned how he and his team had threatened an unnamed AI exec with jail, and after that, the exec “totally changed.”

This Congressional investigation into Nvidia’s China sales and its dealings with Trump is the second one, so far, since news of the alleged spy affair broke. The Republican-led group involves leaders from the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, the House Committee on Foreign Affairs, and the House Intelligence Committee. That group has demanded that Huang come to them and testify in a closed-door session. They had threatened to subpoena him if he didn’t voluntarily agree. On Thursday, after his resignation, he agreed.

Edgewell (EPC) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Restructuring costs and lost service income shape the fiscal 2027 outlook.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Edgewell Personal Care Company (EPC -1.41%) concluded fiscal 2026 by focusing on portfolio simplification and a strategic shift in its commercial investment model. Management reported that the company is moving toward an always-on marketing strategy, increasing advertising and promotion spending while reducing its reliance on short-term promotional couponing. The company is currently executing a multi-site manufacturing consolidation within its Wet Shave segment, a project that is now expected to deliver cost savings starting in fiscal 2028. Strategic objectives for fiscal 2027 include scaling digital growth at primary North American retailers and leveraging international distribution to drive mid-single-digit growth in those markets.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to the Edgewell's Fourth Quarter and Fiscal Year 2026 Earnings Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note this event is being recorded. I would now like to turn the conference over to Mr. Chris Gough, Vice President and Investor Relations. Please go ahead, sir.

Chris Gough: Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us this morning for Edgewell's Fourth Quarter and Fiscal Year 2026 Earnings Call. With me this morning are Rod Little, our President and Chief Executive Officer; and Fran Weissman, our Chief Financial Officer. Rod will kick off the call and then hand it over to Fran to discuss our 2026 results and fiscal 2027 outlook. We will then transition to Q&A. This call is being recorded and will be available via replay on our website, www.edgewell.com. During this call, we may make statements about our expectations for future plans and performance.

This might include future sales, earnings, advertising and promotional spending, product launches, brand investment, investments in technology, advanced analytics and AI-enabled capabilities, organizational and operational structures and models, cost mitigation and productivity efficiency efforts, savings and costs related to restructuring actions, business trajectory and performance improvement, future growth and value creation, macroeconomic and geopolitical factors such as changes in tariffs and conflicts or acts of war, changes to our working capital metrics, currency fluctuations, commodity costs, inflation, future plans for return of capital to shareholders, the disposition of our Feminine Care business and more.

Any such statements are forward-looking statements for the purposes of the safe harbor provisions under the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995, which reflect our current views with respect to future events, plans or prospects. These statements are based on assumptions and are subject to various risks and uncertainties, including those described under the caption Risk Factors in our annual report on Form 10-K for the year ended September 30, 2025, and as may be amended in our quarterly reports on Form 10-Q filed with the SEC and our annual report on Form 10-K for the year ended September 30, 2026.

These risks may cause our actual results to be materially different from those expressed or implied by our forward-looking statements. We do not assume any obligation to update or revise any of these forward-looking statements to reflect new events or circumstances, except as required by law. During this call, we will refer to certain non-GAAP financial measures. These non-GAAP measures are not prepared in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. A reconciliation of the non-GAAP financial measures to the most directly comparable GAAP measures is shown in our press release issued earlier today, which is available at the Investor Relations section of our website.

This non-GAAP information is provided as a supplement to, not as a substitute for or as superior to measures of financial performance prepared in accordance with GAAP. However, management believes these non-GAAP measures provide investors with valuable information on the underlying trends of our business and allows the more meaningful period-to-period comparisons of ongoing operating results. With that, I'd like to turn the call over to Rod.

Rod Little: Thank you, Chris, and good morning, everyone. Fiscal '26 was a year of meaningful progress and change at Edgewell. We are proud to have delivered on our objectives, and we ended the year with strong momentum across the business, supported by a return to organic sales growth, improvement in North America, strength in productivity and returns on our investments in our priority brands, all while navigating a dynamic macro environment with increasing inflationary pressures and a complex manufacturing consolidation project in our Wet Shave business. Fourth quarter organic net sales increased 3.1% with growth in both North America and International.

Gross margin expanded by more than 500 basis points compared with the prior year, and we also delivered meaningful growth in adjusted EBITDA and adjusted earnings per share. For the full year, we grew organic sales and increased both adjusted earnings per share and adjusted EBITDA by more than 6%. Importantly, the improvement was not driven by a single initiative, customer or brand. It reflected broader progress across the portfolio and provides evidence that the investments we have made and the actions we have taken are strengthening our brands, capabilities and business performance. While we still have work to do, fiscal '26 provided growing evidence that the changes we have made are working.

We enter fiscal '27 with stronger momentum and our focus now is on scaling that progress. Let me walk you through four factors that give us confidence in the path ahead. First, we have fundamentally improved our ability to grow revenue. Over the last several years, we have strengthened our leadership team, increased accountability and improved our capabilities across consumer insights, analytics, revenue growth management, brand building and customer execution. These investments strengthen how we plan and allocate resources across the business. Sustainable performance ultimately depends on consistent execution across brands, customers, channels and markets, and we are seeing more consistency and seeing it more broadly across Edgewell than ever before.

We are also seeing that improvement show up with important customers and channels. In North America, for example, Walmart returned to growth after several years of declines, supported by accelerating digital growth and increased share. Second, we've created a more focused portfolio and are strengthening our priority brands. Now that Feminine Care has been divested and this is behind us, our portfolio is simpler, allowing us to concentrate investment and resources behind the brands, categories and markets where we see the greatest long-term opportunity. Our brands are now increasingly connected by a common purpose, nurturing, protecting and elevating your skin every day.

From Wet Shave as an essential first step in the daily skin care regimen to Sun Care and Grooming, that focus is guiding where we innovate and invest. During fiscal '26, we increased our focus on advertising, innovation and commercial support behind our global priority brands. We saw encouraging progress against several of our brands. Cremo continued to deliver strong growth, expanding household penetration and distribution and demonstrating the opportunity we see when increased brand support, innovation and stronger retailer partnerships come together. Hawaiian Tropic built on the momentum created by its repositioning, innovation pipeline and increased support. And we continue to strengthen Schick and Billie through focused innovation, improved commercial execution and brand investment.

We are seeing these investments translate into stronger consumption, increased customer support and better marketplace performance. For the full year, we've grown or held value share in approximately 70% of our country category combinations. Specifically in the United States, aggregate branded unit market share increased all 12 months of the fiscal year. We are also changing how we invest. During fiscal '26, we shifted our commercial investment toward building lasting brand equity and significantly reduced our reliance on couponing and other short-term promotional activity. At the same time, we increased A&P dollars by 7%.

In fiscal '27, we expect another meaningful increase in A&P, which we believe will provide more sustainable and consistent always-on media support behind our priority brands throughout the year and emphasize stronger ROI generating revenue management initiatives. Third, our geographic balance provides another source of strength and opportunity. International represents roughly half of Edgewell's sales and has a long track record of attractive growth. We continue to see significant opportunities across our international markets, supported by strong brands, attractive category positions and the opportunity to expand distribution. International returned to growth in the fourth quarter with broad-based growth across key markets and brands, and we expect mid-single-digit organic growth in fiscal '27.

In North America, the changes we have made are gaining traction. Our branded consumption growth exceeded aggregate category growth, total and branded dollar share improved, and we exited the year with good momentum. Together, we believe North America and International provide a more balanced and durable foundation for growth moving forward. Fourth, we are accelerating the transformation of our operating model. We anticipate that fiscal '27 will be another important year of change for Edgewell with two areas of focus: progressing the consolidation of our North America Wet Shave manufacturing footprint and accelerating the broader transformation of how we operate.

On Wet Shave, bringing new capacity online at the same time we are seeing an accelerated growth profile has been more challenging than anticipated. While we have made considerable progress here, our priority in fiscal '27 is to manage the consolidation at a pace that supports network stability and consistent customer service. As a result, the consolidation timeline has shifted by about six months compared with our original plan, and we now expect the associated savings to begin in fiscal '28. Our broader transformation extends well beyond manufacturing and is not simply an SG&A initiative.

During fiscal '27, we intend to simplify structures and processes, strengthen procurement and end-to-end planning, sharpen resource allocation and increase our use of technology, advanced analytics and AI-enabled capabilities. This work is intended to improve speed and agility, enhance decision-making and create a structurally lower cost base. In fiscal '27, we expect these savings to more than offset the remaining stranded costs from the Feminine Care divestiture, reflected in lower SG&A with a greater contribution in the second half of the year. This transformation goes beyond the cost reduction program.

We are creating capacity to invest more consistently behind our brands and innovation to drive growth while improving the structural economics of the business and our ability to absorb external headwinds without compromising our strategic priorities. These actions are built into our fiscal '27 plan, which brings me to our outlook for this year. Looking ahead, the results we delivered in the second half give us confidence in the direction of the business as we enter fiscal '27. We expect organic net sales to grow between 1% and 3% with growth in both North America and international.

Our plans include an important pipeline of innovation and brand-building initiatives across our key brands, supported by faster innovation cycles and stronger commercial activation, which we believe will continue to strengthen our competitive position and support our organic growth objectives. We also expect adjusted operating profit to grow by approximately 10%, driven by organic sales growth, higher gross margin and lower SG&A from our productivity and transformation initiatives. Importantly, we expect to deliver this growth while absorbing significant energy-related inflation and tariff costs, all while making another meaningful increase in A&P behind our brands.

Below operating profit, our earnings growth is also expected to absorb the anticipated expiration of TSA income from the Feminine Care divestiture and a higher tax rate. We believe we are entering fiscal '27 with a stronger, more focused organization and a growing belief that changes we have made are gaining traction, demonstrated by improving consumption trends, stronger share performance across several key brands and categories and increased customer support for our innovation and brand investment plans. Together with our broader transformation agenda, these proof points reinforce our confidence in Edgewell's ability to deliver more consistent growth and margin expansion over time.

With that, I'll turn it over to Fran to discuss our fourth quarter and full year results and fiscal '27 outlook in greater detail. Fran?

Francesca Weissman: Thank you, Rod. As Rod outlined, the fourth quarter marked another important step forward in our transformation with organic net sales growth in both North America and International and strong adjusted EBITDA and adjusted EPS growth. I'll now walk through the quarter in more detail on a continuing operations basis, beginning with top line performance, then the P&L and our outlook for fiscal '27. Organic net sales increased 3.1% in the quarter as strong performance across Grooming, branded Wet Shave was partially offset by declines in Sun and Skin Care and private label Wet Shave.

North America organic net sales increased 3.3%, driven by double-digit Grooming growth and mid-single-digit growth in branded Wet Shave, partially offset by declines in Sun Care and private label Wet Shave. International organic net sales increased 3% with growth across all segments. Wet Shave organic net sales increased 1.6% as strong growth across the branded portfolio was partially offset by lower private label sales associated with the anticipated disruption stemming from our supply chain consolidation. Encouragingly, branded Wet Shave growth accelerated during the quarter with growth in international markets as well as in North America as improved performance across our focus brands and commercial initiatives in the U.S. are beginning to gain traction.

In U.S. razor and blades, category consumption increased 130 basis points. Our U.S. branded share improved in the mass channel, particularly at Walmart and Target, where we've concentrated our resources and commercial investment. Billie continued to perform well, reaching an 11% share overall with even stronger positions at Walmart and Target. Sun and Skin Care organic net sales increased 6.5%, driven by strong North America Grooming performance and mid-single-digit international performance. Cremo and Hawaiian Tropic delivered strong results in the quarter, supported by increased distribution, innovation and brand investment. Cremo grew more than 50% in the quarter, making it its eighth consecutive quarter of greater than 20% growth.

In the U.S., Sun Care category consumption grew approximately 3% in the quarter, our value share grew 20 basis points, while our unit market share grew 70 basis points, including 280 basis point share growth in Hawaiian Tropic, partially offset by market share declines in Banana Boat. Looking at the category for the year, consumption increased by 2.5%, and our overall market share was essentially flat and generally in line with our expectations, while unit share grew by 90 basis points. Turning to the P&L. Adjusted gross margin increased 510 basis points compared with the prior year, driven by structural productivity as well as lapping transitory costs in last year's fourth quarter.

Strong productivity savings, better volume absorption, favorable pricing and currency more than offset elevated inflation, net of tariffs and unfavorable mix. While the improvement was strong, gross margin came in slightly below our expectations, largely due to higher-than-anticipated inflation, including rising diesel freight and oil prices during the quarter. A&P expenses were 11.6% of net sales, up from 9.8% last year as we continue to step up in brand investment we outlined earlier in the year, including support behind Wilkinson Sword, Schick, Billie, Cremo and year two of our Hawaiian Tropic campaign. This reflects our shift toward more sustained full funnel brand building.

Adjusted SG&A was 20.6% of net sales compared to 21.9% last year, driven primarily by lower corporate and people expenses in the current year. Adjusted operating income was $51.1 million or 10.6% of net sales compared to $27.7 million or 5.9% of net sales in the prior year, primarily reflecting the impact of higher gross margins and lower SG&A expenses, partially offset by higher A&P investments. GAAP diluted net earnings per share from continuing operations were $0.23 compared to $0.26 in the fourth quarter of fiscal '25. Adjusted earnings per share from continuing operations were $0.78 compared to $0.52 in the prior year quarter.

Currency favorably impacted adjusted EPS by $0.11 in the quarter, offset by a $0.35 unfavorable income tax headwind. Adjusted EBITDA was $76.8 million, inclusive of a $6.3 million favorable currency impact compared to $43 million in the prior year. Now let me turn briefly to our full year results. Overall, we delivered full year results in line with our expectations. Organic net sales for the year increased 30 basis points. Our Sun and Skin Care portfolio grew 3%, fueled by nearly 13% growth in our Grooming brands. Sun Care declined slightly, highlighted by a weaker-than-anticipated international sun season in Europe and Latin America.

Wet Shave declined about 1% as approximately 2% growth in branded Wet Shave was more than offset by roughly 10% decline in private label due to anticipated supply chain issues previously discussed. By geography, North America organic net sales increased approximately 40 basis points, primarily driven by volume. International grew 20 basis points as net pricing gains were partially offset by lower volumes, mainly due to a weak sun season in LATAM and Europe and transitory events, including the Middle East conflict and supply constraints. Adjusted gross margin rate was broadly flat year-over-year.

Productivity savings of approximately 230 basis points, favorable pricing as well as net currency tailwinds were offset by approximately 250 basis points of core inflation, inclusive of tariffs and unfavorable mix. A&P expense was 12.2% as rate of sale, an increase of 60 basis points over the prior year as we continue to step up our investment behind our brands. Adjusted operating profit decreased $11.7 million or approximately 7% and adjusted operating margin for the year was 8.1%, down approximately 70 basis points in rate of sale. The decrease in adjusted operating income margin was primarily attributable to the higher brand marketing investment, higher SG&A expense and slight gross margin declines.

Important to note that Fem Care divestiture stranded costs are reflected in both operating profit and EBITDA, while the TSA income offset is only reflected in EBITDA. Adjusted earnings per share from continuing operations were $1.93 compared to $1.81 in the prior year. Currency favorably impacted adjusted EPS by $0.20 in the year, offset by $0.30 of unfavorable income tax headwind. Adjusted EBITDA was $254.5 million, inclusive of an $11.5 million favorable currency impact compared to $239.7 million in the prior year.

Net cash provided by operating activities was approximately $50 million for fiscal '26 compared to approximately $118 million last year, primarily due to lower earnings and the impact of the cash flow headwinds associated with the Fem Care divestiture. As a reminder, cash flow is presented on a consolidated basis for both continuing and discontinued operations. Adjusted free cash flow was $86 million, which adjusts for onetime cash flow impacts associated directly with the Fem divestiture, including tax on proceeds, working capital and deal-related fees and expenses. Adjusted net debt leverage was 3.4x. We continued our quarterly dividend payout, declaring a $0.15 per share dividend for the fourth quarter and returned approximately $7 million to shareholders via dividend.

Now turning to our outlook for fiscal '27. As Rod noted, the progress we made in the second half gives us confidence in the underlying trajectory of the business as we enter fiscal '27 even as we navigate a dynamic external environment. Our outlook builds on that momentum with expected organic net sales growth across our segments and markets, gross margin expansion and growth in adjusted earnings. We expect to deliver this performance while further increasing investment behind our focused brands with A&P that is higher and sustained throughout the year.

Accelerating our operating model transformation will lower SG&A with savings more than offsetting the remaining stranded costs from the Fem Care divestiture and supporting both brand investment and earnings growth. Our outlook also absorbs elevated inflation and the expiration of the Fem transition service agreement income, which underscores the strength of the underlying plan. Now to the details for fiscal '27. We expect organic net sales growth of 1% to 3% with growth in both International and North America. Currency is expected to be about a 60 basis point headwind to reported net sales. For the first quarter, we expect organic net sales to be flat to down approximately 1%.

This reflects an anticipated timing shift of North America Sun Care sell-in from Q1 into Q2 as well as continued pressure in private label related to the Wet Shave consolidation issues we discussed throughout fiscal '26. We expect the capacity-related pressure to be the greatest in Q1, moderating significantly in Q2 with private label returning to growth in the second half. As we look to adjusted gross margin, we expect approximately 60 basis points of year-over-year expansion in fiscal '27. The improvement will be driven by productivity, procurement and transformation savings, our strengthening revenue management initiatives and modest price increases. These benefits will be partially offset by higher costs across the supply chain, including substantial energy-related inflation.

Our outlook for oil incorporates the EIA outlook with the expectations that prices will begin to stabilize as the year progresses. In terms of phasing, we expect gross margin rate to be roughly flat in the first half with expansion building in the second half. We expect the inflation impact to be relatively consistent across the year, while productivity, volume absorption and strategic revenue management benefits will build as the year progresses. We expect A&P to increase in both dollars and rate, up about 80 basis points to approximately 13% of net sales.

We expect the increase will be concentrated in the first half, including a step-up in Q1 to approximately 14% of net sales as we move to an always-on investment cadence behind our brands. We expect adjusted SG&A to improve by 90 basis points as a rate of sales as savings from our transformation initiatives more than offset the remaining stranded costs from the Fem Care divestiture. We expect these savings to build throughout the year with a greater contribution in the second half. Adjusted operating profit margin is expected to increase approximately 70 basis points as gross margin expansion and lower SG&A more than offset higher A&P investment.

Adjusted EPS is expected to be in the range of $1.85 to $2.15 per share, growing approximately 3.5% at the midpoint of the range. This outlook absorbs approximately $0.20 of expected headwinds below operating profit, including $0.17 from the expiration of the TSA income and $0.03 from the higher effective tax rate of approximately 24%, up about 1 point from fiscal '26. Adjusted EBITDA for fiscal '27 is expected to be in the range of $250 million to $270 million, which is approximately 2% growth to prior year at the midpoint of the range, which absorbs the loss of approximately $11 million of TSA income.

In terms of phasing, we expect to generate 2/3 of adjusted EBITDA and approximately 80% of adjusted EPS in the second half. This reflects the anticipated concentration of our A&P increase in the first half as well as transformation savings that build throughout the year. We expect Q1 adjusted EPS to be below the prior year, primarily reflecting the Q1 A&P step-up and higher SG&A expenses. Adjusted free cash flow for the year is expected to be in the range of $100 million to $120 million, which includes the cash impact of restructuring-related costs and adjusts for working capital changes and other items related to the Fem Care divestiture.

As we accelerate the transformation of our operating model, we expect to incur restructuring and related charges of approximately $55 million in fiscal '27. Our capital allocation priorities remain unchanged. We remain focused on reducing leverage in the near term while continuing our quarterly dividend and share repurchases, which will primarily offset dilution. For the specific guidance ranges, I would refer you to the press release issued earlier today. With that, I'll turn the call over to the operator for Q&A.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] And our first question for today will come from Nik Modi with RBC Capital Markets.

Nik Modi: The outlook, would love to -- just get your level setting on the consumer, kind of what you're assuming in that guidance? And then what we've seen historically is when a company goes from struggling to like regaining momentum, it really energizes the trade. And so I just was hoping you can give some thoughts around shelf space and how you're thinking about that next year.

Chris Gough: Nik, this is Chris. You cut out in the beginning of the question. We got the… second part of that first. If you could repeat, please.

Nik Modi: Yes, absolutely. Just on the consumer backdrop, kind of what you're embedding in your assumption for the year.

Rod Little: Nik, so I'll take those in order. Look, we're assuming our categories perform at about the rate they have performed over the last 6 to 12 months. When you look at our aggregate category growth rates past 13-week, 26, 52-week, pretty steady. There's maybe been a slight decline in volume trends, but on a price-based level, very steady. So we're planning that going forward. We have low growth in the categories with a relative continued outperformance. If we get to the top end of our range, midpoint suggests we hold share is essentially what we're looking at.

We are very careful as we look at price, price components. we're going to work revenue management, price per unit, price per pack very hard because we still are seeing a difficult price environment in our categories, either consumers that are stretched very sensitive to price and retailers effectively not open for price as they compete with each other. So overall, I think I would say what we've seen over the last 6 to 12 months, we expect to continue. That's what's built into our guidance. And on the second point, you're absolutely right, as you start to win with consumers and shoppers, retailers look at you differently. They start to get behind you in different ways.

As you know, we've been on a 1- to 2-year journey to improve our standing with retailers, improve our brand support. We're spending now 3 points of sales more in marketing and advertising support than we were in fiscal '24. We're at about 10% rate of sale. Then our guide outlook, as Fran said earlier, contemplates 13% of sales. And so retailers are seeing that. Consumers are responding to that. And what's transpired in the year just finished and what we think will continue to happen as we get into '27 is net positive distribution outcomes, more space, higher quality, better shelf positioning for in-cap off-aisle displays, and that is a global read. That's not just a U.S. view.

So that gives us confidence as we go into this year that we've got retailers seeing the progress we're making and rewarding us with those types of outcomes.

Operator: The next question will come from Peter Grom with UBS.

Peter Grom: Rod, I wanted to just ask specifically about the long-term margin opportunity. And look, you touched on the various actions you're taking across SG&A, plant consolidation, and I know there's a lot of moving pieces here and timing considerations. But could you maybe just unpack how you think about the long-term margin opportunity here. Clearly, expecting another impressive year despite a lot of external headwinds. But it seems like just based on your commentary, there's just more to come. So just curious if you can give us some perspective on that as we look out to maybe '28 and beyond.

Rod Little: Yes. Peter, look, we're in a structurally positive cycle as we look at gross margin development and being able to accrete and grow gross margin from here. Sorry if there's some noise in the background. There's three levers to the margin play. The first is pricing, revenue management, and we feel like we've got a very strong plan as we look at all components of pricing. And that may not be list price to consumer changing as much as it's looking at all of our spending between gross and net sales and optimizing that and getting that to work harder for us.

The other part of pricing that I would call out is as our brand health improves, better consumer metrics, higher household penetration, better resonance of content that our teams are building and putting in front of consumers, better market share outcomes, 70% of our category country combinations either holding or growing share. That also gives you an opportunity on the price line that, frankly, we just didn't have one or two years ago. So price is a big lever. Cost of goods is a big lever as well. As you look at the cost of goods line, the shave consolidation is a big piece of that. That's all in front of us and to come.

And then the final piece is the leverage we should get on the SG&A line, not only in our ability to manage that line to get leverage on scale as we grow, but see some absolute cost reductions as you are seeing in our implied guide for fiscal '27. So all three of those levers will contribute. We won't call out a long-term target. We've got one in mind that we're going for. But again, I think a lot of confidence that we can build margin from here and have levers to offset if we face higher inflation or tariff costs from here.

Peter Grom: Super helpful. And then maybe as a follow-up and more near term, when you speak to substantial inflation, is there a way to unpack specifically what you're expecting or embedding in the guidance for this year?

Francesca Weissman: Yes. So I think when we think about inflation and when we look at how we're exiting in Q4, we've got a combination of core inflation, you've got the annualization of tariffs, and we've got oil and commodity prices increasing more in line with what we're seeing right now in terms of oil prices and what the EIA outlook has been, which is roughly around the mid-80s. So we'd expect more pressures in the first half easing in the second half. And as we cycle next year, overall inflation is about 300 basis points as we look at all of those components.

And I think what mitigates that is well over 250 basis points of productivity and volume absorption as we're increasing our overall units. We're getting better capacity against our plans, and we're really focused on price and revenue management, which will help to mitigate and balance the overall gross margin profile and put us in a position where we're expanding year-over-year.

Operator: The next question will come from Chris Carey with Wells Fargo Securities.

Christopher Carey: Just first on the private label dynamic. Can you just give us a sense of how this headwind evolved through fiscal '26, perhaps give us some idea of the quarterly cadence and how impactful it was? I think I heard kind of a way to frame it for the full year, but I just want to understand how it evolved through the year. And then what's your visibility on this supply constraint improving? And just give us a sense of how that's going to look North America versus international? Then I have a follow-up.

Rod Little: Yes. Chris. So look, all the private label dynamics and supply constraints are being driven largely by the new plant consolidation effort that we're running in Wet Shave. Again, from a strategic point of view, it's a very important project for us. This is a massive cost unlock, scale, automation, all the good things that will go with this over time. So we're in the early phase of that journey, roughly 50% of the way through that project of four plants consolidated into one new greenfield site. As it relates to the fiscal '26 plan, we were cutting orders from the beginning of the year in North America and private label.

And to put context to it, in the year just finished '26, private label was down about 10% in Shave, branded up about 2%. And so it was the drag, and it was supply constrained driving that much more than consumer demand or impact. What you see though is we did pick up some of that decline in growth in branded. But we were cutting in North America all year from the beginning of the year. As we talked, we reduced the tail, cut out low gross margin customers across the year to focus on our most profitable customers. That's actually a positive for us as we go forward.

As we hit into Q3, that's really when it peaked out where we were cutting also in Europe, and you saw that in our reported results. The good news as we exit the year, what you see is we're coming up the curve on production output. There's less constrained lines. You saw the improvement in Q4. particularly in the European business where we've returned to growth as that production output came online. And we have good line of sight to what we built this year being quarter 1, really the quarter that's most impacted by that constraint.

As we get into Q2, Q3, Q4, we lap it and we actually have growth planned in the back half of '27 on the private brands business. So we know where we are. We don't love where we are, but we feel good about where we are. And the other thing I would point out as we go forward here, the branded business has been very strong. And so as we get into the back half of the year and we get this unlocked, we'll be in a good place, I think, on the shave portfolio.

The final piece of this is we've applied all the learning, obviously, from the first two sites have been consolidated in for the last two sites to go. We're underway with the transfer of the last two sites, so that's moving forward. And I think we've appropriately phased the savings out of fiscal '27 into our fiscal '28. So the guide contemplated has no savings from that program hit it, and that will then begin to come in fiscal '28, '29.

Christopher Carey: Okay. All right. Great. Sustainability of some of your faster growth areas of the portfolio, including Grooming, can you just give us a sense of how the pipeline looks in the near term? And maybe also, if you could just comment on your two largest brands in Sun Care and how you see some of that market share developing into next year given momentum for one is quite a bit better than the other right now? That's it for me.

Rod Little: Yes. So on the faster growth areas, Cremo is leading the way, 50% in the quarter. past eight quarters, 20-plus percent growth. So there's an acceleration in that brand. It's driven by innovation, new product launches, better distribution, outcomes that we do expect to continue. And that's now broad-based strength in that brand, not only across beard car, which is growing in a declining segment. That was the heritage of the brand when we bought it. Body -- body washes in particular, fast growing. More recently, adds into antiperspirant deodorant as we expand the range across there. So we view Cremo growth is very durable and a lot more to come there.

Bulldog's, the other brand I'd call out, fast growing, done really well for us across the European markets. Again, great innovation, really nice pipeline and really tight focus on the marketing message and the target we're going after there. Hawaiian Tropic, Banana Boat, very different outcomes in fiscal '26. Hawaiian Tropic grew almost 300 basis points of share across the year increasing household penetration, all the consumer metrics where you want as we put investment behind that business in the year past. As we look to '27, we think we'll continue to have good momentum on Hawaiian Tropic.

And the big news is going to be on Banana Boat, which we expect to go from share decline into more of a share hold position as we relaunch the brand, brand-new packaging restage, retailers, consumers have been very positive on the reaction to that, a new campaign, great content with that. And so I think Banana Boat, which was a leaky bucket in '26 will not be as we look to '27.

Operator: The next question will come from Susan Anderson with Canaccord Genuity.

Susan Anderson: I guess maybe just a follow-up on that. And then also just any updated thoughts on M&A to maybe add some faster-growing brands to your portfolio in your core areas?

Rod Little: Susan, we have a lot of innovation going out across the portfolio. I won't be specific to mention like specific products or changes that we're making, but there's a whole suite of innovation coming out across Cremo and our Grooming brands. Same with our Sun Care brands, we've got some new launches and formulations coming under Hawaiian Tropic and Banana Boat both next year. What I haven't talked about yet is Shave. We've got some really compelling innovation. We've announced and it's already launched, just launched actually last month in Japan, Shick First Acne Safe. So this is extending on our Schick First Tokyo launch, which we launched over a year ago.

We're now coming with an upgrade and an add to that line with a razor that is the safest razor ever put on the market and works well with acne from skin. And that just launched, and we've got a good investment behind that to drive that and really good distribution outcomes behind that as we look across Japan and then more broadly across Asia. Domestically, in the U.S., we've got some launches of some technologies and brands we've had other places, primarily on Schick as we look into what we're launching here. Billie will be the focus point of new innovation that we're bringing to market this year on the Billie brand.

Again, I'm not going to be specific on what that is. But as we get into the spring planogram resets, you'll see that roll out. And it's the biggest innovation we've had coming on the Billie line since we acquired it a few years ago. So we feel good about the broad-based innovation we have being something that year-over-year should be accretive to our growth rate as we look at the total innovation plot for the year.

Susan Anderson: Okay. Great. And then maybe just on Sun and Skin, it sounds like Grooming was the big driver there. Maybe I missed it, but if you could just talk about performance of Sun across North America and international. I think the sellout according at least to Circana in North America have been pretty strong. So just curious how the sell-ins have been for you there and how the season inventory ended at retail.

Rod Little: Yes. Overall, I'll start and then, Fran, you build. What I'll say is Sun Care internationally was weak, primarily weather in Europe and Latin America were not great. The Sun Care category here domestically in the U.S. was just okay. It was pretty wet. It did grow. There's better penetration. But I would say from an overall category weather perspective, kind of an average to below average year that we're cycling.

Francesca Weissman: Yes. And Susan, I would just say, when we look at U.S. Sun, we broadly delivered low single-digit growth, which was what we intended, broadly flat share, and we were up 90 basis points through the year. Of course, there's puts and takes between the quarters. We had really strong consumption in Q4, where quarter share was up about 70 basis points. So in terms of retail inventory, we feel really good that consumption has been strong and our sell-in has been strong.

As Rod talked through, most of the weakness that we saw in Sun did come from weather-impacted areas in LATAM and in Europe, but we remain really confident with our performance in the U.S., and that's been in line with expectations.

Operator: The next question will come from Olivia Tong with Raymond James.

Olivia Tong Cheang: Can you compare and contrast your performance in Shave versus Sun and Skin for this year? And then where you think the bigger rate of change will be next year? So how do you think about sort of the rate of change there? Clearly, this year held back a bit by the weather, unfortunately. But you also have some changes in terms of formulations across the category coming in '27. So thinking about how you plan for that.

Francesca Weissman: Yes. Thanks, Olivia. So let me just take your questions in two parts. One, to just wrap up the sun season for this year in Hawaiian Tropic and Banana Boat. We always anticipated some share declines in Banana Boat, and that was factored into our outlook. Broadly, overall share performed in line with what we expected. So Hawaiian Tropic was up about 280 basis points, some declines in Banana Boat, but overall unit share up about 90 basis points. And when we think about how we delivered the year, we returned to top line growth. We saw that consistently coming through branded Wet Shave, still low single-digit growth. When we think about Sun, Grooming was high single-digit growth.

So when we think about segment performance, we really see strong branded segment performance. And specifically, even in the U.S., our core brands that we've been focusing on was up around 4%. So while we do see that North America has stabilized, we see that in our core brands, we're actually performing in line with expectations. That's up low single digits, and we're seeing that contributing to our overall branded share trajectory, which is up about 40%. So we feel really good about the investments that we're making and the improving proof points that we're seeing around brand equity. As we look across '27, we see that continuing.

We anticipate that branded wet share -- sorry, branded Wet Shave will continue to grow, and we will continue to hold and gain share in those markets. Sun and Skin will also continue to grow. We see that with a combination of growth both in Sun and in Grooming, where we anticipate Grooming will be around high single digits. The only drag in terms of overall growth profile for the year next year will be a little bit private label, where we expect some declines in the first half and that returning to growth.

And that's what helps us get to the phasing of half 1, we're at the low end of the range, half 2 at the high end of the range. And that really also supports Rod's earlier point around distribution as a lot of our innovation is going to be ramping up in half 2.

Rod Little: And Olivia, I'd just build on this to summarize it, we've known each other for a while here. But what's different as we look to '27 versus any time in the recent past is the depth and breadth of the growth profile we have. Grooming is growing rapidly. We have both Sun, Skin and Schick growing next year, right? That's our projection. So we have all segments growing. We've got a very balanced spread now between international and North America with both international and North America growing. So whether you look brand segment, geography, everything is growing. And that's what's different.

We've gotten rid of the leaky buckets, some of it via M&A choices and a lot of it via just improvement in terms of how we run our brands and the teams that run those brands. And so that's the piece that looks different. We have everything contributing to growth from here.

Olivia Tong Cheang: Got it. And then can I ask a follow-up in terms of advertising? You mentioned about 13% next year. Can you -- what do you think is the right level of ad spend relative to your sales over the long term? Is that 13% kind of where you think it should be? Or is there a possibility that it even goes higher from there as you focus more on the branded initiatives?

Francesca Weissman: Thanks, Olivia. I think when we look at overall A&P spend, we've been intentionally taking a step-up over the last couple of years to make sure that we are delivering sustainable brand equity across our brands. And that step-up has been intentional and considerable over the last 18 months. In half 2, we disproportionately increased our A&P investment to really support that return to growth trajectory. And now as we enter into fiscal '27, you're going to see disproportionately more increased A&P in the first half because that has been a deliberate choice to ensure that we are all consistently throughout the year and always-on media approach across all our brands.

So as we exit fiscal '27 into '28, we're going to see better leverage coming out of our A&P spend, where we're going to have consistency in growth and the step-up in terms of what we're seeing over the last two years, we're starting to moderate. As we think about absolute rate of sales, we're not targeting an absolute target. We're making sure that we are improving in terms of our ROI across these brands, and we're focused around investing around the right aspects for brand building and a better balance between our coupon spend and our commercial investment.

I think the last piece you'll see is some of the improvement that we've seen in gross margin has come from intentionally focused on revenue management. We've talked about throughout the year where we're balancing more around lowering our coupon spend and shifting that spend to upper funnel activity. We've seen that hold traction. We've seen that improve our brand equity scores, our household penetration. So I think what you'll also continue to see as we move forward is just a better balance of commercial investment between point of purchase and also brand building and upper funnel.

Operator: As there are no more questions in the queue, I would like to turn the conference back over to Mr. Rod Little for any closing remarks. Please go ahead.

Rod Little: All right. Thank you, everybody. Look, we had a strong finish to the fourth quarter. OfferUp co-founder sees ‘mini power plants’ in buildings full of EVs

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Challenges, breakthroughs and big ideas in the race to protect our planet. SEE MORE

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 12, 2026 at 2:41 pmNovember 12, 2026 at 2:41 pm

Nick Huzar co-founded Juicer Energy in 2023 to make electric vehicle charging more widespread and reliable. Now the Seattle-area startup is pursuing a broader goal: helping buildings manage rising electricity demand and costs.

Juicer installs and operates EV charging systems at apartment buildings, hotels and other properties. It is also developing battery systems to store electricity on-site and help manage the power needed for charging and other building uses.

The push comes as data centers and the growing use of electricity for transportation, heating and other needs put more pressure on the grid. Batteries can help balance supply and demand by storing energy when electricity is plentiful and releasing it when demand peaks. In some cases, they can reduce the need for costly grid upgrades or new power plants built to meet peak demand.

“We think there’s a big opportunity there,” Huzar said. He spoke at an event this week hosted by GeekWire and Bank of America at the Bellevue showroom of Ferrari of Seattle.

Other companies are similarly pairing EV charging with battery storage. Fellow Seattle startup Electric Era has deployed battery-backed fast chargers at retailers including Costco and convenience stores. ADS-TEC Energy and XCharge also sell fast-charging systems with integrated batteries.

Huzar, OfferUp’s co-founder and former CEO, sees an opportunity to make buildings more self-sufficient by combining hardware and energy-management software. He said Juicer has a project scheduled to come online in 2027 with 70 chargers in one building.

His longer-term vision also includes tapping the energy stored in vehicles connected to those buildings.

“Imagine you’re an apartment owner and you have 20 EVs, 40 EVs plugged in,” Huzar said. “That is a huge amount of power. Like you’re a mini power plant at that point.”

He described feeding excess electricity back to the grid as a possibility that remains years away. He also predicted that most businesses, apartments and homes would have on-site batteries within the next decade, in part because battery costs continue to fall.

Juicer started with EV charging, Huzar said, “but the more we’ve gotten into this, the more we realize that we actually can build an asset for buildings versus relying on the grid.”

The Climate Pledge is a commitment to reach net-zero carbon emissions by 2040, co-founded by Amazon and Global Optimism in 2019.

Seven years later, the community has grown to over 700 signatories across 62 industries and 49 countries and territories, reducing emissions, scaling emerging climate solutions, and addressing challenges across operations and value chains.

15 Hotels With Actually Luxurious Indoor Pools

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s hard to nail an indoor pool—they can very easily veer into athletic club territory, where the smell of chlorine is inescapable, and every splash seems to elicit an unwanted echo. Outdoor pools, with their trompe l’oeil effects and decadent sun loungers, often get most of the credit, but some of their indoor counterparts can pull off even better architectural shows. From thermal baths powered by hot springs to top-level tanks with skyline views, the indoor pools on this list are nothing short of spectacular—not to mention, they can be enjoyed year-round, so you won’t miss out when it rains in New York or Paris. Far from your YMCA, these pools have design details you’ll swoon over: skylights, floor-to-ceiling windows, vaulted ceilings, mosaic tiling, and more. Yes, these hotels are worth booking for the awe-inspiring swims alone. Read on for the best indoor pools at hotels around the world, spanning Mexico to Vietnam.

Location: Lake Louise, CanadaThe pool: A heated pool with an adjacent steam room, featuring views of Lake Louise

Nestled in Alberta’s Banff National Park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site, the Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise is surrounded by the best of nature. Clear blue skies, mountain peaks, the Victoria Glacier, and the emerald-colored Lake Louise create a storybook backdrop that’s worth traveling for. One of the newest and most exciting additions to the Fairmont is the 2025 opening of Basin Glacial Waters, an all-sensory thermal bathing facility that fuses nature and science to offer its guests the ultimate indoor-outdoor wellness experience. From cold plunges and steam rooms to indoor-outdoor thermal pools with some of the best views that Lake Louise has to offer, there are restorative options for every individual’s needs. White-glove service, a cutting-edge wellness space, and once-in-a-lifetime vistas await. —Meaghan Kenny

Location: New York City, New YorkThe pool: A 65-foot, heated, Japanese-inspired sanctuary

In the four decades since Aman was founded and its first property opened its doors in Phuket, the brand has come to represent a certain kind of experience for a certain kind of traveler: discreet, expansive, and intimate, with a full-bodied approach to wellness (one of the best spas in New York) and an almost chameleon-like ability to adapt to the surrounding landscape. Aman New York is the brand’s very first urban outpost in the United States and only its second urban property in the world after Aman Tokyo. The Beaux Arts landmark was carefully renovated by Belgian designer Jean-Michel Gathy of Denniston Architects; the space dazzles with a 7,000-square-foot outdoor terrace, a spa and wellness center spread over three floors, and serene guest suites (each with its own fireplace). You can swim laps in the magnificent 65-foot pool, or post up on one of the poolside chaises, which are interspersed with fireplaces. —Betsy Blumenthal

Location: Chicago, IllinoisThe pool: A heated pool with floor-to-ceiling windows offering views of Lake Michigan and the Chicago River

When the undulating tower that is the St. Regis opened in 2020, it became the tallest building in the world by a female architect (Jeanne Gang)—a title it still holds. And the wait was worth it: Glorious city, lake, and river views; a stellar hotel gym; two restaurants; and a world-class spa with an indoor pool and outdoor terrace bring a new dimension to urban luxury. Though the tower sits right above the Chicago River, the space between its circular driveway and Lake Michigan is a very posh (and quiet) residential area called Lakeshore East. The wellness floor includes a 12,000-square-foot spa and boasts an athletic club with a full fleet of state-of-the-art equipment, daily and group fitness classes, plus a dedicated Pilates reformer studio. —Heidi Mitchell

Location: Mexico City, MexicoThe pool: A heated saltwater pool on the 38th floor, adjacent to the Cityzen Rooftop Bar

The Sofitel Mexico City Reforma is a contemporary oasis to both imbibe and reset along Mexico City’s most enigmatic avenue. The property features 275 guest rooms, of which 56 are suites—for something truly special, book the Imperial Suite, which features floor-to-ceiling windows that look out onto the iconic Angel of Independence monument. The expansive urban spa (probably the best in the entire city) has hot and cold dipping tubs, both a steam and sauna room, and—best of all—a heated indoor pool, set one floor below the spa on the 38th level, where you can relax post-treatment with an unmatched view of Mexico City. But the real star of the property is Cityzen, a rooftop restaurant and cocktail bar that offers the perfect perch to view the city from above. —Michaela Trimble

Location: Torres del Paine National Park, ChileThe pool: A glass-fronted, heated indoor infinity pool located within the Uma Spa

Tinged red at dawn, the granite towers of Torres del Paine are a blazing centerpiece in Chile’s premier national park. But no view compares to the scene that greets guests from their bed at this exquisitely crafted lodge. Barely visible along the skyline, the lakeside building sinks comfortably into the landscape, giving all rooms a view onto the soaring peaks. Light pours through glass walls to illuminate a panoramic restaurant and spa with a heated indoor infinity pool, complete with a hydro-massage and water jets—watch the natural towers play hide-and-seek as clouds breeze by, and keep an eye out for guanacos and rheas, which come close to the lodge. Far from the crowd-clogged trails, this coolly unassuming property invites endless possibilities at the bottom of the world. —Sarah Marshall

Location: Cadenabbia, ItalyThe pool: Part of the Longevity Spa, an indoor vitality pool featuring floor-to-ceiling views of Lake Como

The Lake Como Edition merges palatial grandeur with the Edition group’s signature cutting-edge design. In a destination saturated with old-school addresses, this hotel might be exactly what Italy’s grandest lake needs to inject a dose of contemporary cool. Located just over an hour’s drive from Milan airport, the Lake Como Edition is housed within a restored 19th-century palazzo, and sits on the lake’s western shore in the small but charming village of Cadenabbia, just steps from the water’s edge. Across the glassy expanse, postcard-perfect Bellagio stretches into view, as ferries glide hypnotically back and forth between the shores. A standout feature of the hotel is the Longevity Spa: Facilities include an indoor pool, steam room, herbal sauna, and plunge pools, all set within a deeply tranquil space. Following a meticulous four-year renovation that was completed in 2026, creative directors Kirstin Bailey and Paul Haslhofer have carefully preserved the building’s historic bones while introducing a more contemporary aesthetic.—Lucy Bruton

Location: Bad Ragaz, SwitzerlandThe pool: A 19th-century thermal pool fed by the Tamina Gorge waters

In 1242, a bird hunter in the Tamina Gorge discovered a pure hot spring with healing minerals and a consistently toasty temperature of 97 degrees. Visitors have been traveling here to take advantage of the thermal water’s healing properties ever since. Today, that “blue gold” is one of many draws of Grand Resort Bad Ragaz in eastern Switzerland, about an hour’s drive from Zurich. Comprising four interconnected hotels, the resort offers guests two thermal spas, saunas, steam rooms, and hammams, as well as its signature NewYou Method: A holistic, evidence-based approach that uses the latest findings in medicine and wellness to help each individual. For dining options, you’ll find at least six Michelin stars among the seven restaurants, including the three-Michelin-starred Sven Wassmer Memories. There, Chef Wassmer uses the Alpine region as his seasonal pantry. —Caitlin Gunther

Location: Berlin, GermanyThe pool: A 65-foot-long indoor pool, part of a 19th-century bathhouse

Hotel Oderberger's setting is one of the most splendid in Berlin: It's inside Prenzlauer Berg's landmarked 19th-century Stadtbad Oderberger, a public bathhouse designed by architect Ludwig Hoffmann, who also created the city’s iconic Pergamon Museum. The conversion to a boutique hotel preserved many of the grand building’s original details, most notably the 65-foot-long indoor pool, set beneath a magnificent vaulted stone ceiling and surrounded by massive arched columns and huge, church-like windows. It's the first thing you see when you enter the lobby, and it definitely impresses. The 72 rooms are all unique in layout, and the modern design incorporates historic details in a clever way: Original numbered wood doors from the bathing cabins, now glassed in, live on as bathroom doors. Go for the cool historical details and great location in the heart of buzzy Prenzlauer Berg. —Jennifer Ceaser

Location: Furnas, PortugalThe pool: A thermal spring pool fed by heated volcanic waters

Octant Furnas offers a calming, restorative escape from the already serene São Miguel, the largest island of the Azores archipelago. You’ll feel taken care of at every moment, and the scenery is some of the most beautiful you’ll ever lay eyes on. It occupies the structure that was once the old Furnas Thermal Center, and while the Furnas volcano last erupted in 1630, the geothermal activity remains around both the hotel and the entire town of Furnas. That means the indoor pool is filled with naturally heated thermal water, and yes, one dip will melt all your troubles away. The hotel boasts 54 rooms of various sizes with something for parties of every size; every room is spacious, incorporating small details like Marshall speakers, and comes with robes, slippers, and sun hats for enjoying the pools. The central meeting place of the hotel is the signature restaurant À Terra, with its beautiful outdoor seating area, glass roof, and hanging plants. —Kate Kassin

Location: Cádiz, SpainThe pool: Part of the on-site spa, a light-filled indoor pool overlooking the gardens of La Alcaidesa

Oku Andalusia brings the wabi-sabi cool of its Ibiza and Kos siblings to a sprawling beachfront resort on Cádiz’s quieter stretch of the Costa del Sol. Its 255 rooms and suites pair earthy minimalism with thoughtful design details; families and groups will particularly appreciate the impressive 72 suites, private-pool options, and four-bedroom villa. Not to mention there’s a world-class kids club and extensive activity programming for toddlers to teens. Yet adults aren’t forgotten: Two child-free pools, DJs, and wellness experiences create a grown-up counterpoint. Even if you’re not booked in for a treatment, make time for the spa’s indoor pool. It is an arch-filled architectural delight, and offers a serene contrast to the cool Balearic-house soundtrack of the main pools. Oku is that rare, family-friendly resort where couples and child-free guests can still enjoy a more grown-up experience. —Bridget Mills-Powell

Location: Paris, FranceThe pool: Paris’s longest indoor pool, featuring handcrafted mosaics and digital depictions of the city skyline

Skip the sleepy districts that house so many of Paris’s plush hotels, and book a private perch with a bird’s-eye view of the river and La Samaritaine as your fashion-forward neighbor. From a sophisticated suburban crowd to the “bobos” (bourgeois-bohème) of the right bank, every type of Parisian rubs shoulders with jet-setting guests in the convivial seventh-floor Art Deco brasserie-meets-cocktail bar, Le Tout-Paris. It’s the place to be seen before you escape to the subterranean spa or your room with a view. More than half of the 72 elegant rooms, 46 of which are suites, overlook the Seine. Tucked away below ground, the brightly lit spa, designed like a modern living room, is helmed by another member of the LVMH équipe and star of both Parisian couture and beauty: Christian Dior. The nearly 100‑foot pool is said to be the city’s (and continent's) longest hotel pool; it’s designed by architect Peter Marino, situated under a mirrored ceiling, and surrounded by handcrafted mosaics by Michael Mayer and large-scale digital screens that create the effect of looking out at the Seine and Paris’s skyline. —Madison Flager

Location: Oyama, JapanThe pool: A 59-foot-long, groundwater-fed pool with a view of Mount Fuji

Practically every window, every sliding door, and every gap between the trees at Gora Kadan Fuji frames Japan’s most iconic peak, Mount Fuji. The mountain may be the star of the show here, but there’s a lot more to love when it’s shrouded in clouds. Gora Kadan Fuji’s 39 suites and three villas feature tatami, granite, paper, and cypress in a flawless blend of traditional Japanese design and contemporary comfort. Service, delivered by attentive staff in exquisite kimonos, is calm and caring. Breakfast is a succession of delicate, locally sourced morsels presented in pretty little dishes. Convivial dinners take place at the seven-seater counter of Kappo and at equally bijou Sushi Fuji Takumi, where seafood from nearby waters is transformed into seasonal nigiri. Guests soak neck-deep in communal and private onsen baths, filled from underground springs and snowmelt groundwater. The spa also has a 59-foot indoor pool with Fuji views and a small outdoor infinity pool, as well as a 24-hour gym. Gora Kadan Fuji is a place to return to in every season. —Nicola Chilton

Location: Hanoi, VietnamThe pool: One of the largest pools you'll find at any hotel in Hanoi, with views of the skyline

The brainchild of architect Carlos Zapata, whose striking design draws inspiration from Vietnam's coastline and is meant to look like a dragon rising beside the water, the 450-room JW Marriott Hotel Hanoi has become one of the capital's defining business hotels. Its generous rooms and warm, intuitive service make it a rewarding retreat from the city's frenetic center, while standout dining—from the upscale French Grill to one of Hanoi's most expansive breakfast buffets—ensures there's plenty of reason to stay in. Suspended on the eighth floor beneath soaring floor-to-ceiling windows, the enormous indoor lap pool has the scale to make swimming a proper part of your stay rather than an afterthought. The surrounding wellness complex includes a well-equipped fitness center, sauna and steam rooms, and the Spa by JW. For travelers who value space, comfort, and a quieter setting, it's an easy recommendation. —Dan Q. Dao

Location: Rabat, MoroccoThe pool: A year-round saltwater pool located within the spa

A shiny, super-sized proposition from Four Seasons, this palatial hotel successfully incorporates beautiful Moroccan touches in the shared areas and sleek luxuries in the private spaces. This would be an ideal three- or four-night stop for a bit of sightseeing and a lot of pampering as part of a wider Moroccan tour, with Rabat being a serene introduction to what is a very colorful and frenetic country. The true headliner here is the spa, which has thorough and high-end treatments for every mood, with the opportunity to relax by the magnificent indoor pool afterwards (here, you can either get your laps in, or recline in a hot tub or steam room with bottled water or even Moroccan mint tea brought to your lounge chair). There’s an effortless blending of old and new, and a devotion to looking after the guest. —Matt Charlton

Location: Melbourne, AustraliaThe pool: Located on the 64th floor, a heated infinity pool offering views of Melbourne’s skyline

When booking a stay down under, why not go to the very top? The Ritz-Carlton, Melbourne is Australia’s tallest hotel, housed in the top 17 floors of an 80-story building. The hotel’s unprecedented vantage point envelops guests in panoramic views of the city and the ocean lapping at Port Phillip Bay. If you find the views hard to pull away from, the in-house restaurant Atria, helmed by chef David Green, serves a seasonal five-course tasting menu for dinner, showcasing modern Australian cuisine at its freshest, with seats right at the window. With door-to-door valets and a Ritz Kids program where story time brings local Australian culture to life, the standard of service is as sky-high as the rooms. The chic, modern pool sits on the 64th floor, offering 24/7 access to water with an excellent view over the city.

Microsoft global outage: What the company has said so far

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:39 p.m. ET — As of this writing, the global Microsoft outage remains ongoing.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 3:55 p.m. ET — We've updated this story with the latest statements from Microsoft.

Microsoft is having a really bad Thursday.

Starting in the early afternoon, Microsoft 365 enterprise software products experienced a major outage, which is ongoing at the time of publication. On the platform DownDetector, more than 100,000 users reported problems with Outlook alone.

The company has posted regular updates about the situation, both on social media and on its website.

On the company's official status page (which also went offline briefly, according to multiple Mashable reporters), Microsoft said that Exchange Online, Teams, SharePoint Online, Copilot 365, and Defender XDR were all experiencing issues. The status page also indicated that the outage is only affecting Microsoft's enterprise software, Microsoft 365, not its consumer products.

Mashable has reached out to Microsoft for comment, but the company hadn't responded at the time of publication.

In an initial update posted at 12:54 p.m. ET to X, the company said that customers were having problems with Exchange Online mailboxes "in the Americas." Exchange Online is a cloud-based tool that provides back-end support for Microsoft Outlook.

However, by 1:07 p.m. ET, the company acknowledged the problem was more widespread, affecting more services and regions. "While we're working to mitigate impact, we've confirmed the issue may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region," the company posted.

The Microsoft Status page was then updated with a notification that stated, "User impact: Users may be unable to connect to multiple Microsoft 365 services." The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

While a root cause has yet to be determined, Microsoft has at least shared a brief technical explanation for what's going on.

"We've determined that the elevated load isn't driven by an overall increase in traffic, but by a large number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable, which is shifting load onto the remaining infrastructure," Microsoft said on its status page, as well as in a thread on X. "We're investigating what's causing these servers to become unavailable while we work to safely return them to service."

Later on in the X thread, Microsoft said it had not identified any recent change that correlates with the outage, but "the failed health checks may originate from a specific component that's responsible for processing server health." In the meantime, Microsoft has worked to prevent more servers from being impacted while it also tries to resuscitate the ones that are down.

Shortly before 4 p.m. ET, the Microsoft status page received another update, stating that it is still troubleshooting and restarting servers.

"While validating the targeted change, we found that many of the affected servers have become unresponsive, which is preventing us from confirming whether the change is effective," Microsoft said. "We're restarting these servers so they can come back online with the change in place, and we're applying the same approach to additional affected portions of our infrastructure to validate the results more broadly."

By the evening, Microsoft's updates turned slightly more optimistic, as the company stated that some servers had already come back online. A 4:00 p.m. update stated that the company had "validated an alternative service health check method, which allowed a subset of affected servers to successfully return to service."

However, by 5:09 p.m., the company acknowledged that services remained offline.

"We’re opening all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief," read an update posted to its website.

How many billions are at stake if Jensen and Lori Huang divorce?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There are plenty of reasons Jensen Huang's resignation is a big deal. Not only does his departure leave Nvidia, the AI chipmaker he co-founded 33 years ago, looking for a new permanent CEO for the first time. It also puts the spotlight on the Huang family, whose ties to the company run well beyond its founder.

To catch you up, Huang stepped down four days after first telling employees he'd been unfaithful to his wife of 41 years, Lori Huang. He made the admission after reports of a relationship with Alice Huang, a businesswoman, who first of all is unrelated to him, and whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for China. On Wednesday, the New York Times reported that he'd also had a relationship with Kimberly Powell, an Nvidia executive who reported directly to him. And then on Thursday, hours after resigning, Reuters reported that Huang had an "intimate relationship" with another Nvidia employee, whom the outlet did not name.

To make matters worse (if possible), the Huangs' two children, Madison and Spencer, both work at Nvidia, making this whole scandal a family affair with a far more personal question at the center of it: What would happen to the Nvidia multi-billion dollar fortune if they divorced?

Of course, this analysis doesn't mean the couple is divorcing — Lori hasn’t made any public comment. She and Jensen are deeply connected in business as co-trustees of three trusts that directly held nearly 669 million Nvidia shares in March. A September SEC filing shows those direct holdings at about 608 million shares, with additional stock held through companies their living trust owns. Nvidia says the couple may share voting and investment power over those shares.
