Weekly Mortgage Rates Drop to 6.99%, but Don’t Exhale Just Yet

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Although mortgage rates are hovering around 7%, an expected Fed hike could send them up.

For the first time since the Federal Reserve raised its benchmark rate in September, weekly mortgage rates averaged below 7%.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage fell two basis points to 6.99% APR in the week ending Nov. 12, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

Mortgage rates have been relatively stable after the midterm elections, but recent inflation data hints that this may not last.

The midterm elections were a big question mark for markets, and mortgage rates were elevated through much of October. Since the results were announced, mortgage rates have hovered around 7% — about 30 basis points below October’s average.

Housing affordability was a key platform issue for many newly elected officials, and some went beyond rate talk to propose ways to expand affordability as a whole.

For example, Sen.-elect James Talarico of Texas wrote on his campaign website that he wants to “create a federal task force charged with helping communities use quality, cost-effective options like factory-built ‘modular’ homes to speed up construction timelines.”

This proposal echoes the goals of the bipartisan legislation that lawmakers passed this summer, which expanded standards and access to financing for manufactured housing.

We anticipate that mortgage rates will rise as we get closer to the Dec. 8-9 Federal Reserve meeting. Central bankers are overwhelmingly expected to raise borrowing costs, and traders reinforced that forecast after the Bureau of Labor Statistics dropped the Consumer Price Index for October on Nov. 10.

The report showed that inflation grew 0.4% in October, higher than economists’ predictions of 0.3%.

Inflation has remained above the Federal Reserve’s 2% target since 2021, even after the central bank raised the federal funds rate in September. The federal funds rate is the interest that banks pay to borrow money overnight, which is how they pay for investments like mortgages. It’s also the main tool in the Fed’s kit for supporting a healthy economy through monetary policy.

In theory, when the federal funds rate goes up, inflation should come down. Mortgage rates are very likely to rise before a federal funds rate hike, since lenders adjust ahead of time when they anticipate higher costs.

Probably not soon, since it’s so likely that the Fed will raise borrowing rates in December. But be on the lookout for headlines like these, which could lead to lower mortgage rates:

Iran war winds down. Democrats now have a majority in both houses of Congress, and Rep. Hakeem Jeffries of New York — expected to become the new Speaker of the House — has affirmed that ending the war is one of the party’s chief priorities.

“We have to end the reckless and costly war of choice. This is a war that we should have never gotten into,” Jeffries told NY1 on Nov. 11. Any perceived successes on this front could potentially lead to falling mortgage rates, since the war with Iran has been such a major driver of inflation.

AI profits soar. AI companies have been borrowing enormous amounts of cash. Goldman Sachs projects debt associated with hyperscale data centers to reach $420 billion by 2027. These firms represent 40% of credit increases across the industry.

This borrowing has driven up bond yields, and, consequently, mortgage rates. If these businesses start to generate real profits, they could use more of their own cash reserves and borrow less.

Inflation falls. If inflation drops (which would be the Fed’s goal with a federal funds rate hike), so would mortgage rates. Lower inflation would make it cheaper for mortgage lenders to generate loans, and would make it less likely that the Fed would have to raise rates again later.

Here’s what happened in crypto today

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Nvidia shares plunged after co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO and left the board. Coinbase’s head of EU policy said tighter DeFi rules could push users toward riskier platforms. Binance’s security chief warned about fake job interviews months before attackers used a similar tactic in the exchange’s $6.3 billion hack.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as president and CEO and stepped down from the company’s board on Thursday, effective immediately. The board named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Kress will retain her role as CFO while Nvidia’s board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent CEO role. The board also appointed Harvey C. Jones its first chair and said it expects Nvidia’s operations to proceed without interruption.

Nvidia shares fell more than 13% shortly after the US market opened on Thursday, trading at $151.00 as of 2:31 p.m. UTC, according to Yahoo Finance. Bitcoin (BTC) fell alongside Nvidia shares, dropping as much as 3.5% to a multimonth low.

The shares later pared losses to trade at $165 at 4:24 p.m. UTC, down 5% on the day. The stock was down more than 26% over the previous month.

European Union efforts to tighten oversight of decentralized finance (DeFi) could drive crypto users toward platforms offering fewer investor protections, according to Coinbase’s head of EU policy.

Roeland Van der Stappen told Cointelegraph that regulated crypto platforms could respond by limiting or dropping access to DeFi services.

“Rules designed to protect investors would end up pushing activity towards the places with the least protection,” Van der Stappen said.

In its September response to the European Commission’s consultation on the Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), the European Securities and Markets Authority recommended creating a new category of regulated crypto services for firms that give customers access to DeFi protocols.

Months before Binance lost $6.3 billion to a hack, its chief security officer described to Cointelegraph how a fake job interview could give attackers a foothold inside the exchange — closely resembling one of the entry methods later used against the exchange.

During a July interview about Binance’s response to rising security threats, Jimmy Su told Cointelegraph that fake job interviews were “something we worry about a lot internally,” explaining how attackers could tailor a job opportunity to an employee before persuading them to run code as part of a technical assessment.

“And once that gets executed, the device is compromised and then they can start harvesting credentials,” Su said, adding that attackers could then use that to penetrate further into the network.

Su’s detailed warning about malicious coding assessments was not included in Cointelegraph’s July coverage, but takes on new significance following the biggest crypto exchange hack in history.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cerebras Systems reported better-than-expected revenue and quarterly guidance in its earnings report on Thursday, and touted a new deal with Microsoft. The stock soared 17% in extended trading.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Cerebras' core revenue grew 70% from $135.7 million, compared with 103% growth in the second quarter, according to a statement.

"I think there is opportunity created by, particularly on the hardware side, the sale of hardware, where long lead times in GPUs and more expense creates opportunity for us," Cerebras CEO Andrew Feldman told CNBC in an interview, referring to graphics processing units.

Executives will announce a new relationship with Microsoft, one of the world's top cloud providers, on a conference call with analysts, Feldman said. The call starts at 5 p.m. ET.

"We believe revenue begins to show up in Q1 '27," Feldman said in the interview, adding that Microsoft signed the deal in October. Cerebras has also landed deals with other major cloud providers Amazon and Oracle.

Cerebras is evaluating its financing options to meet the ramping demand, Feldman said in the interview.

The company is trying to challenge Nvidia in the AI chip market. Its earnings land on the same day that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned from the company he co-founded after multiple scandals involving extramarital affairs.

For the fourth quarter, Cerebras expects a core gross margin of 47% to 49%, with $310 million to $330 million in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for a 42.1% margin and $271 million in revenue.

Cerebras said it expects revenue to triple in 2027. That implies about $2.85 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $2.92 billion.

Cerebras shares have slumped by about 47% since the company debuted on Nasdaq in May.

Widespread Microsoft outage takes down Outlook and other 365 services

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft suffered a widespread outage on Thursday that impacted thousands of users across regions, according to outage-tracking website Downdetector.com.

The outage began shortly before 1 p.m. and impacted Microsoft 365 services, Outlook and Teams. At the time, about 130,000 users reported issues with Outlook.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site.

"We are aware and investigating reports of users experiencing issues with Microsoft 365 services, primarily issues with Exchange Online," a Microsoft spokesperson wrote in a statement to CNBC. "We appreciate our customers' patience while we work to restore services as soon as possible."

Microsoft shared updates on the outage from its Microsoft 365 Status account on social media platform X.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m., Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

Microsoft initially said infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions" was responsible for the connectivity problems.

The company later determined that the elevated load was caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure" and that some servers were failing automated health checks.

"We've taken steps to prevent additional servers from being impacted while we work to return the affected servers to service," Microsoft wrote.

Alice Huang tied to Nvidia’s biggest Chinese competitor

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang may have owned stakes in businesses in China that benefited Nvidia's biggest competitor in China, Huawei, at the same time that she was in business with Nvidia and also reportedly having a romantic affair with its ousted CEO Jensen Huang.

The connection between Alice Huang and Huawei was first discovered by analysts at private intelligence firm Tesari. The firm exclusively shared its findings with CNBC. Tesari, which uses artificial intelligence to collect evidence and specializes in national security investigations around the world, provides intelligence research to private companies and the U.S. government.

This previously unreported entanglement may raise questions about Alice Huang's intent and conduct in her relationship with the CEO and whether Jensen Huang knew his romantic partner was closely connected with his competition. Jensen Huang has acknowledged having an affair, though the precise timing of his relationship with Alice Huang is unknown.

The mysterious saga of Alice Huang involves a tangle of interlocking corporations and transactions that show her connection to entities that ultimately provided computing power to Huawei in China.

Huawei is known as a Chinese government "state champion" in the technology and telecommunications sectors, meaning it receives enormous financial support from the government in Beijing so it can lower prices and undercut global competitors. It is considered a key part of the Chinese government's push for global technical dominance and is closely linked to the Chinese Communist Party.

Businesses connected to Alice Huang were linked with supplying Nvidia computing power to Huawei, according to documents reviewed by CNBC, around the same time she and Jensen Huang were first publicly seen together, in December 2023.

In 2024, Nvidia said Huawei was a top competitor, specifically in artificial intelligence chips including graphics processing units, central processing units and networking chips.

"This activity, which surfaced through our analysis of Alice's network, warrants a national security and [technology] counterproliferation investigation," said Brian Andersen, Tesari's vice president for national security applications and a former supervisory special agent with Homeland Security Investigations.

If the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry Security and Homeland Security Investigations found evidence that anyone transferred "controlled equipment" to Huawei without authorization, potential charges might include export-control violations, conspiracy and smuggling, Andersen said.

The connection between Alice Huang and Huawei is so convoluted that analysts at Tesari told CNBC that based on their previous investigative experience, they suspect it was designed to deliberately obscure the relationship.

Nvidia and Huawei did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment. Jensen Huang did not respond to a voicemail left on a mobile phone. Alice Huang could not be reached.

According to Chinese corporate records and media reports compiled by Tesari and provided to CNBC, Alice Huang, whose Chinese name is Huang Le and who according to documents at the time was based in Hong Kong, in 2019 sold a cloud business she owned called Osmanthus to a Chinese gaming company called 7Road. A subsidiary of 7Road, which was based in Singapore, was spun off as "Megaspeed" in 2023. Megaspeed would go on to become a key Nvidia customer, helping facilitate at least $7.5 billion in Nvidia chip shipments to Chinese firms, The Information reported Tuesday.

As early as 2016, Alice Huang had also become a co-owner of a financial firm known as Shanghai Hexi, which heavily invests in tech and data centers. In 2021, Hexi in turn acquired 27 percent of a company called Shanghai Century Longteng, a data center developer, which Hexi held until 2024. That firm owned a controlling 50.1 percent stake in a data center facility called Shenzhen Yifeng Technology Co. Ltd. in mainland China.

The key customer of that data center was Huawei Cloud, the Shenzhen, China-based subsidiary of Huawei Technologies.

In addition, Alice Huang's firm Hexi guaranteed the loans required to finance the Shenzhen data center, which did business with Huawei.

Hexi did not immediately respond to a message via its LinkedIn page sent outside of normal business hours.

CNBC translated corporate records and Chinese news articles in Mandarin that Tesari provided.

It's not clear if the Shenzhen data center was able to acquire Nvidia GPUs, but its majority owner began purchasing the chips in 2023. That company is known as Zhejiang Century Huatong Group and is a business partner of Alice Huang's Hexi through their joint ownership of Century Longteng.

That made Alice Huang a part owner of a data center providing computing power to Huawei. And that was happening at around the same time the first known sightings of Alice Huang and Jensen Huang in public together.

And that raises the question of why Huawei, which has access to Chinese-made chips, would need to rent computing power from an outside entity.

Hexi exited its stake in the data center development company in 2024, but individuals within Alice Huang's network are still connected to the Shenzhen data center. Her husband Kang Wei is a longtime business associate who founded Hexi, where Alice Huang previously held an executive role. In 2022, Kang became an executive of Century Longteng, which owns the controlling stake in the Shenzhen data center, and he also attended the company's signing of the Huawei agreement.

DuPont Partners with Microsoft and NobleAI on Agentic AI for R&D

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DuPont announced on November 12, 2026 a research collaboration with Microsoft and NobleAI that will broaden access to AI and machine-learning tools for its scientists and engineers, with the goal of reducing the time needed to deliver customer solutions.

Each company contributes a different component. DuPont supplies expertise in advanced solutions and applications; NobleAI provides Science-Based AI modeling through its Visualizations, Insights and Predictions (VIP) Platform; and Microsoft contributes Discovery, its enterprise agentic AI platform for R&D. DuPont’s stated goal is to help teams assess potential solutions faster, guide experiments and accelerate cycles of learning.

The proposed workflow separates research orchestration from scientific modeling. Aseem Datar, Microsoft’s corporate vice president of product innovation for Microsoft Discovery & Quantum, described Discovery as an agentic environment that connects R&D teams’ data, models and know-how for scientific discovery. NobleAI CEO Alex Wang divided the software roles between Discovery’s planning and orchestration of research and VIP’s predictive and optimization modeling.

Wang described a workflow in which DuPont teams would use their existing data, expertise and processes to explore more ideas in silico, meaning through computer simulation, before concentrating physical testing on the candidates most worth pursuing. That sequence places AI earlier in candidate selection: models help narrow the options, while physical testing remains part of development rather than being displaced by simulation.

NobleAI’s modeling approach is designed for situations where industrial data is limited. The announcement describes Science-Based AI as integrating scientific principles throughout the modeling workflow, VIP as turning limited industrial data into predictive models, and NobleAI as one of Microsoft Discovery’s first launch partners. That relationship brings the modeling approach into agentic workflows for industrial R&D.

DuPont is linking the technical work to a company-wide AI rollout model. Chief Information Officer Matt Abbott said, “This collaboration reflects how we are scaling AI at DuPont, with security and governance designed in from the start, so what proves out in one lab can be put to work across the company.”

That approach treats a successful laboratory result as a candidate for broader use, with security and governance forming part of the deployment design. Chief Technology Officer Marty DeGroot said the combined tools are expected to accelerate development, shorten time to market and expand innovation’s impact across DuPont’s portfolio. DuPont serves healthcare, water, construction and industrial markets, giving that portfolio-wide ambition a broad potential scope.

The practical test is whether this combination can shorten development cycles while producing results that hold up outside the initial laboratory. If it does, the governance model Abbott described would provide a route for turning a successful local workflow into a broader R&D capability across DuPont’s portfolio.

Neal and Wyden Open Probe Into Nvidia’s China Chip Sales

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard E. Neal and Senate Finance ranking member Ron Wyden opened an investigation on November 12, 2026 into the Trump administration’s approval and taxation of Nvidia H200 AI-chip sales to Chinese companies, including a 25% tariff, and demanded that relevant records be preserved.

The letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick sets out a transaction chain: H200 chips manufactured in Taiwan would be imported into the United States for a security review, assessed the tariff, licensed by Commerce for export, and then shipped to China. That sequence is central to the inquiry because it combines an import tariff with permission for an onward export.

Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962 permits presidential action against imports determined to threaten national security. Neal and Wyden argued that using it in this arrangement appears to create an unconstitutional export tax, effectively taking a cut of Nvidia’s China sales while benefiting Nvidia and the Chinese government and undermining U.S. security.

Their committees’ jurisdiction over taxation and tariffs gives the investigation a trade-law focus alongside the export-control questions. The inquiry therefore concerns both whether the chips should reach Chinese buyers and how the administration structured and taxed the transactions.

Commerce is being asked to preserve material concerning its Section 232 semiconductor-import investigation and resulting tariffs, its review and approval of Nvidia’s export-license applications, and its communications with or about Nvidia, former chief executive Jensen Huang, and their agents or representatives. The request covers existing and future records and includes a Section 232 report that, according to the letter, Lutnick allegedly sent Trump on December 22, 2025.

The lawmakers warned that federal criminal laws prohibit destroying records relevant to congressional investigations. Their demand makes the department’s internal deliberations, including how buyers were reviewed and how the tariff was constructed, part of the new inquiry.

Licensing diligence is another focus. Citing press reports, Neal and Wyden said Commerce approved sales to Chinese companies with government and military ties, including cutout companies with no physical presence at their listed addresses. They also repeated reports that Commerce suspended a separate investigation into Nvidia customer Megaspeed after outreach from Nvidia.

The letter, citing press reports, describes Alice Huang as a Chinese executive in a close relationship with Jensen Huang and as the leader of Megaspeed, a company suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips into China. U.S. intelligence officials identified her as a Chinese intelligence asset and concluded that she funneled information to the Chinese government and facilitated Chinese access to advanced Nvidia chips.

The letter says Trump and Lutnick were reportedly briefed on that intelligence, but Trump nevertheless granted Nvidia permission in December 2025 to sell H200 chips to China in exchange for a cut of export sales.

Neal and Wyden said the approval came only after Jensen Huang and Nvidia directed at least $26 million toward Trump’s personal priorities, including the White House ballroom. They questioned whether, and to what extent, political considerations improperly influenced the administration’s policy decisions.

A separate November 9, 2026 letter from House Foreign Affairs Democrats Gregory W. Meeks, Gabe Amo, and Sydney Kamlager-Dove sought an immediate halt to advanced AI-chip exports to China. They also asked Commerce to explain what officials knew about the reported intelligence connections, when senior officials were informed, and why exports continued.

That earlier appeal emphasized the chips’ importance to AI capability, military modernization, and economic competition. It warned that once those capabilities reach China’s government or entities acting for it, the consequences could be nearly impossible to reverse.

The earlier letter centered on stopping future exports. Neal and Wyden’s demand also puts the approval process itself under scrutiny, including whether tariff powers and political considerations displaced national-security judgments.

Everyone’s selling XRP right now, but why?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

XRP [XRP] has been sliding on the charts for weeks, and it looks like everyone’s selling their stash right now.

Whales have been steady though. They’ve been trading big, but we don’t quite know whose side they’re on.

Cryptoquant’s Futures Taker CVD has been sell-dominant since late August. That hasn’t changed yet so, two and a half months straight.

The only time this year Futures traders leaned buy-heavy was in the beginning of 2026. In that time, XRP has gone from $1.55 in late September to $1.05 now.

Spot traders used to turn a blind eye, but not anymore. For the most part, their CVD was neutral this year. There were some buy-heavy stretches in January, February and May.

That changed in October. Now, the Spot Take CVD is also sell-dominant. This is the first time that’s happened all year!

That matters more than you think. Futures selling might just be traders betting short. When Spot starts selling too, that would mean that people holding actual XRP may be getting out.

Usually, when people trade a coin this much, it’s because the price is rising. With XRP though, that’s not been the case.

Futures volume bubble map went red in late August. Through most of September and early October, it was marked as “overheating.” While that’s slowed down a bit since, it’s still been a fair spike.

It moved into “heating” in early September, was “overheating” in the middle of the month, and has stayed hot ever since.

Now, put that next to when Spot volume was “cooling” at $1.40.

Both markets are busy right now, but since it’s all during a dip, it may be indicative of selling.

Finally, CryptoQuant’s Average Order Size has shown “big whale orders” on Futures and Spot since early October.

That’s happened before as well. Whale-sized orders were all the rage through June, July, and most of August. All this, while XRP went between $1 and $1.25.

Towards the end of August, the price went back to $1.50 on the charts.

As with many others, this metric also only shows how big orders are, and not which way they’re going. Whales could be buying the dip, or walking away.

Either way, they’re the ones moving the most money right now. Whatever they decide at $1 is probably where XRP goes next.

Unemployment Labs Reports Blackwell Patterns in Chinese AI Models

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Unemployment Labs analysis by Will DePue, published November 12, 2026, found initialization patterns consistent with NVIDIA’s export-controlled Blackwell GPUs in six open-weight models from DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group.

The analysis associates DeepSeek-V4-Flash and MiniMax-M3 with initialization on B200 or B300 chips, and DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Kimi K3, Kimi K3.1-Pro and Ling-3.1-flash with GB200 or GB300 chips. The matches identify the hardware layout behind the starting weights; whether those chips also performed training is a separate inference.

Successive releases place the apparent changes in time. DeepSeek-V3 produced a Hopper reading in December 2024, followed by the two Blackwell-reading V4 models in April 2026. Moonshot AI’s Kimi K2.5 read as Hopper in January 2026 before K3 in July 2026; MiniMax-M2.7 and M3 followed the same pattern in April 2026 and June 2026, respectively; Ant Group’s Ling-3.0-flash and Ling-3.1-flash did so in August 2026 and October 2026.

The pattern was not universal. The newest readable models from Alibaba, Zhipu, Tencent, Xiaomi and StepFun produced Hopper rather than Blackwell readings.

The technique begins with weights, the numerical parameters that training adjusts. Before training, the PyTorch/cuRAND setup examined uses the Philox generator to produce repeatable values from a seed. GPU threads distribute those values among weight positions, and a sufficiently large initialization uses 2,048 threads per streaming multiprocessor (SM). Changing the SM count therefore changes where the same generated numbers land.

In the configurations tested, Hopper H100 SXM, H200 and H800 chips have 132 SMs and 270,336 threads; B200 and B300 chips have 148 SMs and 303,104 threads; GB200 and GB300 chips have 152 SMs and 311,296 threads. A reading identifies one of these groups, not a specific chip within it.

Weights reserved for words or symbols absent from training can preserve their initial values almost exactly, while trained weights may retain a weaker statistical relationship to those values. The investigators select a seed and layout on one set of weights, then test that unchanged combination on a separate set excluded from the search.

Under its statistical model, the report estimates that roughly 6 trillion seed-and-layout combinations would produce an expected strongest chance match of about 8 sigma, a statistical measure of distance from random variation. The strongest results for the identified models range from 49 to 1,385 sigma, and every individual test included reaches at least 20 sigma.

DePue’s physical-chip reproductions provided another check. Using DeepSeek’s recovered seed, an H100 reproduced DeepSeek-V3’s preserved values but not V4-Flash’s, while a B300 did the reverse. On the B300, unmodified PyTorch reproduced 461 of 469 selected V4-Flash rows with correlations above 0.99, including 445 complete bit-for-bit row matches. A rented GB200 reproduced all 13 preserved Kimi K3 rows with Moonshot’s seed; changing either recovered seed by one produced no matching rows.

The report says the method agreed with all 24 cases where a developer publicly named a specific model’s hardware, while cautioning that 11 involve step-zero checkpoints and related models are not independent. That makes the comparison a consistency check rather than a measured accuracy rate.

Kimi K3.1-Pro uses K3’s seed and may be a K3 checkpoint with further post-training. Assuming the initializing hardware also performed training, DePue estimates five distinct final runs at roughly 500 to 3,000 Blackwell GPUs each, generally over two to three months. The calculation divides training compute by chip throughput at 35% utilization, using DeepSeek’s disclosed token counts and weight-based token estimates for the other models. Applying the same arithmetic to DeepSeek-V3 produced 2,000 to 2,500 chips, compared with DeepSeek’s disclosed 2,048.

A match concerns initialization alone; it cannot inventory every processor used during later training. A lab could create initial values on one system and transfer them, mixed clusters could leave only some hardware traces, and a Hopper result does not exclude subsequent Blackwell training. Nor can weights establish chip ownership, location or acquisition. DePue’s report notes that Blackwell systems can be rented outside China and says this evidence alone establishes neither smuggling nor an export-control violation.

AMD’s MI300X is the identified hardware alternative. It normally uses 304 processing units but can be divided into two groups of 152 or configured to use 148, reproducing the layouts in physical tests. For DeepSeek-V4-Flash, 363 of 377 preserved values that differ between vendors matched NVIDIA and none matched AMD. That numerical test did not settle the other five models, although the report considers a specially configured AMD cluster very unlikely.

The report states that B200, B300, GB200 and GB300 exports to China require a U.S. license and that no such licenses have been reported; it also says the MI300X is controlled. The method itself requires public weights and a recognizable hardware-dependent initialization pattern. CPU initialization leaves no GPU layout, and Baidu’s PaddlePaddle generator does not carry the examined chip layout.

The analysis gives policymakers technical evidence of hardware access without supplying an enforcement conclusion: public weights can expose an initialization pattern, but determining whether export controls were breached would still require evidence about where the hardware was used, who owned it and how access was obtained.

You Could Learn to Pilot This Flying Car in Just 5 Minutes

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Is it a bird? Is it a plane? Is it a... flying Cybertruck? Nope, it's a modular flying car that will take to the skies next year.

When I first laid eyes on the Aridge flying car at the IFA consumer electronics show in Berlin this past September, I didn’t fully understand what I was looking at.

Taking pride of place on the stand belonging to Chinese robotics company Xpeng, there was a van that bore more than a passing resemblance to a Cybertruck. It didn’t have any wings, and it looked far too dense to fly. Sitting next to it was a kind of oversized drone with a cabin just about large enough to fit two people.

The two vehicles seemed like totally separate entities. It never would’ve occurred to me to assume they were two halves of one system – much less that the drone, with its substantial wingspan, could fold up to fit inside the van.

I now understand that this modular two-part flying car system is known formally as the Aridge Land Aircraft Carrier. The ground vehicle, also known as the mothership, is a chunky 5.5-meter-long van that can be driven with a standard license and fits in most parking spaces. It’s designed to store, carry and deploy the electric vertical takeoff and landing vehicle, or eVTOL, including its six foldable rotors, which can fly as high as a helicopter for 30km (about 19 miles).

This remarkable creation is the work of Deli Zhao, founder and president of Aridge, which is backed by robotics and auto company Xpeng. Speaking at Web Summit in Lisbon on Tuesday, Zhao presented himself as a man who was inspired to pursue a career building flying cars by Harry Potter and the Green Goblin, despite his lack of formal education.

“Dreams are very important,” Zhao said, as a video of him flying the eVTOL played on a screen in the background. Anyone can learn to fly the vehicle with just five minutes of training, he added. Or if you’re too tired to use the simple joystick, the eVTOL can fly itself autonomously.

“Everyone here can be a pilot,” Zhao said. “Everyone can be a captain.”

This sounds like a story from the very kinds of science fiction that sparked the creation of the Land Aircraft Carrier in the first place – and yet, it isn’t. It’s far from the first time I’ve seen a flying car concept at a tech event. They’ve been showing up for almost a decade at this point. But what marks this one apart is that Xpeng says it has already taken 17,000 orders for the vehicle across China, the UAE and other Asian markets.

“Our advantage is we’re closer to commercialization,” Zhao said. Xpeng has established the world’s first mass-production factory for flying cars in Guangzhou, with trial production beginning in November last year. Right now, it’s ramping up capacity and improving quality with the aim of eventually being able to build a flying car in 30 minutes.

Aridge has worked through many different concepts, including flying motorcycles, to finally find one ready for commercial production (plus it has a six-seater air taxi and integrated flying car in the works for the future too). If you have $300,000 going spare, the Land Aircraft Carrier could soon be yours.

The vast majority of preorders are from individuals, according to Xpeng, but the vehicle has also been designed for use in tourism and search and rescue. Zhao said it solves multiple problems with existing eVTOLs simultaneously: storage (it lives in the back of the mothership), recharging (it charges as the mothership is driving), transportation to and from appropriate flight sites, and ease of use.

“I hope to make flying accessible to everyone as soon as possible,” Zhao said. Right now, it takes around 20 to 30 hours of training to acquire the sport pilot license you’d need to fly the eVTOL. In China, Zhao said he’s in conversation with the regulator to reduce this, emphasizing his vehicle’s ease of use.

Zhao said he’s learned a lot from the aviation sector in China, where the Land Aircraft Carrier has gone through extensive safety testing and will likely take to the skies first as a consumer-owned product as soon as next summer. Growing its low-altitude economy, which encompasses drone delivery and eVTOLs, is national policy for the country – in spite of calls for more regulation after a deadly crash in Beijing this summer.

Xpeng is also eyeing the European market, although the regulatory hurdles are likely to be substantial. “Europe’s aviation sector is highly developed,” Zhao said, expressing his respect for the region’s strict rules, but adding that the market was important. The company will also start exploring certification in the US next year, he said. Actual progress will depend on the outcomes, he added.

In spite of Zhao’s boundless optimism, it’s difficult to buy into the hype around his flying car without witnessing it in action in person. I’ve now seen the Land Aircraft Carrier twice in different locations and videos of it in operation, but I want to observe the eVTOL’s deployment from the mothership and its airborne skills for myself.

At one point during his Web Summit talk, Zhao climbed into the cockpit of the eVTOL on stage. For a moment it seemed as though he might fly it right then and there inside Lisbon’s cavernous ME Arena. The audience raised their phones in unison, only to drop them again in disappointment when they realized Zhao wasn’t actually going to take off.

No matter how idiot-proof it might be to fly, it turns out European health and safety rules still apply.

Disclosure: Katie Collins traveled to Lisbon as a guest of Web Summit to serve as a panel moderator. Her reporting from the event was independent of that role.

Bluevua Ropot RO System Review: Trustworthy Tap-to-Table Taste

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 12, 2026 5:16 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

We’ve tested our fair share of kitchen appliances over the years, and for our money, a good water filter is one of the most practical investments you can make. From removing tap water’s harsh taste and smell to catching invisible and unhealthy contaminants, countertop reverse osmosis systems make access to crisp, clean drinking water easy.

In our recent head-to-head testing of the best countertop water filters, the Bluevua Ropot countertop reverse osmosis water filter system earned the top spot. It wasn’t just the five-stage filtration that won us over—it was the combination of NSF- and WQA-certification, the no-installation setup, and the fact that the filters last up to 2 years, all wrapped in a design that won’t look out of place next to your coffee maker.

Performance is perhaps the most important aspect of a water filter, and the Ropot’s is excellent: water passes through a sediment filter, a pre-carbon block, a high-precision reverse osmosis (RO) membrane, a post-carbon filter, and a remineralization stage. Unlike some competitors, Bluevua backs this unit’s performance with actual NSF and WQA certification.

The downside of such thorough filtration is that the process takes longer than it would with a basic pitcher filter, meaning the Ropot isn’t for you if you’re not going to be diligent about refilling it in advance. But if you can get into the habit of refilling the reservoir after every couple of glasses, it’ll be well worth it. Not only will the five-stage filtration process remove contaminants (including sediment, chlorine, heavy metals, fluoride, PFAS, microplastics, pesticides, dissolved solids, and bad tastes and odors), but it goes the extra mile with a remineralization stage that adds beneficial minerals back in, like calcium and magnesium. In our taste tests against tap water, pitcher filters, and other RO systems, the Ropot’s flavor was easily one of the best.

While reverse osmosis systems are hard to beat in terms of filtration performance, they’re traditionally a bit rough when it comes to installation. But since the Bluevua Ropot is a countertop unit, there’s zero drilling or plumbing necessary. You just set it on the counter, plug it in, and fill the removable 1.77-liter tank. The digital panel shows live total dissolved solids (TDS) readings alongside filter-life indicators, so you know when it’s time to change them out. When that time comes, the cartridges twist out and in without tools.

We also loved the high-borosilicate glass pitcher. Some of the others we tested didn’t come with a collection chamber for the filtered water at all, and if they did, they were often plastic (which can absorb odors or degrade over time). Bluevua’s glass carafe keeps purified water fresh and fits neatly in the refrigerator if cold water is what you prefer.

In terms of downsides, the Ropot was one of the more expensive water filters we tested, and the slower flow rate and larger footprint are real considerations if speed or space are your priorities. But between the certified filtration, the 2-year filter life, and water that consistently tasted better than bottled, we think it’s worth it.

Whether you’re shopping for a new homeowner or a serious hobbyist, we’ve got a gift idea for you.

California cities get firm notice: follow new housing laws, or else

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

California housing fights are increasingly happening before a vote, with nonprofits sending letters that cite HAA, density bonus law, and CEQA reforms. The goal is to warn officials and create a record that can be used if a city rejects or reduces a project.  AI Summary

A 135-townhome project cleared the Rancho Cucamonga Planning Commission on Tuesday on the planning department’s recommendation.

That is the outcome state reforms are meant to produce: more housing, including affordable units. Warmington Residential Homes’ project includes seven for very-low-income households. Still, the meeting showed how these disputes now run. Before a decision, a pro-housing nonprofit reminds the city to follow the law, creating a record it can cite if officials ignore the warning.

In Rancho Cucamonga, the California Housing Defense Fund sent a three-page letter citing the Housing Accountability Act, the state Density Bonus Law and last year’s California Environmental Quality Act reform. Rejecting the project or cutting its size, it warned, would require written findings of a specific health or safety hazard.

The Rancho Cucamonga letter was one of at least five CalHDF sent that day, along with letters to Santa Clarita, Whittier, Escondido and Twentynine Palms. Four concerned projects of 17 to 146 units. One addressed an ADU ordinance.

Those letters are a fraction of the total. James Lloyd, the organization’s director of planning and investigations, told HousingWire the number reaches about 100 a year.

They serve as a first step toward making sure cities implement state housing reforms properly, according to the organization.

Broadly, the letters pair legal warnings with a pitch to local officials: new housing helps a city’s finances and existing residents while meeting the state’s need for more homes.

“While no one project will solve the statewide housing crisis, the proposed development is a step in the right direction,” the organization writes.

Lawmakers have spent years trying to speed housing construction to improve affordability. Gov. Gavin Newsom signed Assembly Bill 130 to reform CEQA and SB 131 in 2025, exempting qualifying infill housing from environmental review and narrowing review for projects that nearly qualify. Legislators also tightened the Housing Accountability Act, which limits when cities can deny or shrink projects that meet zoning.

Neighbor suits have not disappeared. They have shifted to different arguments. Attorneys at the legal publisher CEB expect litigation to focus on whether a project qualifies for a CEQA exemption at all, rather than on the adequacy of an environmental report.

Builders and advocates pose a growing risk for cities that resist state law. Cities frequently lose when they test the rules in court, and the losses can be costly. Under Senate Bill 1037, penalties can reach $50,000 per unit for willful Housing Accountability Act violations.

La Cañada Flintridge refused to process a builder’s remedy project. A judge ruled against the city in March 2024, and the city dropped its appeal a year later rather than post a $14 million bond. It paid CalHDF $1,262,500 in fees and costs last year.

A small project in Pacifica shows the stakes. Neighbors sued over an eight-townhome project, arguing it needed a full environmental impact report instead of the lighter review the city used.

A trial court sided with them, rescinded the approvals and awarded nearly $1.3 million in attorney’s fees. In December 2025, an appeals court vacated the fee award, saying the judge had wrongly discounted the project’s housing value. It sent the question back to the trial court.

Neighbors of the Rancho Cucamonga project voiced concerns at community meetings this summer, raising traffic, wall height and privacy. Staff added conditions on construction parking and frosted windows.

But when it came to a recommendation, staff tracked the laws CalHDF cited. They wrote in their staff report that the project qualified for waivers under the state density bonus law, which limits when a city can deny them. Staff also noted the project was exempt from environmental review under CEQA guidelines.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/medical-transport-booking-platform-medhaul-raises-41m-expand" hreflang="en">Medical transport platform MedHaul, focused on high-needs patients, raises $4.1M</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Medical transport platform MedHaul, focused on high-needs patients, raises $4.1M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

MedHaul, a platform for coordinating medical transport, has raised $4.1 million in an unnamed round, Fierce Healthcare has learned exclusively.

The strategic round, which brings MedHaul’s total raised to date to $7 million, was led by Black Ops VC, with participation from Pier 70 Ventures, Seae Ventures, Stanford University, 450 Ventures (BCBS of Alabama), LaunchTN and American Cancer Society’s BrightEdge.

The latest capital will be used to expand deeper in oncology, which represents 60% of rides booked on the platform, and to support a forthcoming Epic integration.

Millions of Americans are affected by transportation barriers to care, and the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services estimates that these barriers account for a quarter or more of missed clinic appointments. Missed appointments are associated with mortality among patients with chronic conditions and cost the system billions annually, according to one 2017 estimate.

MedHaul is a B2B platform working with providers and payers. Its tech helps providers match patients with medical transport according to acuity and accommodation needs, such as a wheelchair or stretcher. Care teams can use MedHaul to schedule and track transportation, with same-day and advanced bookings available. They can also manage billing, communicate with patients and identify those at greater risk of missing an appointment. MedHaul also rolled out a newer clinical trial product designed to support CROs and pharma clients.

The existing medical transport ecosystem is “very antiquated” and “heavily manual,” MedHaul founder and CEO Erica Plybeah told Fierce Healthcare. Many transport companies are small businesses that are not tech-savvy, or that need business development support. When MedHaul first entered the market, in 2019, some vendors didn’t even have a website or a social media page, Plybeah noted. And manual back-and-forths between care teams and ride providers means it can take upwards of two hours just to book a ride.

MedHaul’s network spans 17 states, primarily serving patients in the cancer, kidney disease, maternal health and behavioral health spaces. It also supports hospital discharges and rural patients. It has about 600 transport companies in its network.

MedHaul has also launched an enterprise platform, designed for large health systems and multi-location practices. Through a partnership with Uber Health, ride share and non-emergency medical transport bookings are available in one place, on the platform. The platform also provides real-time analytics into patient mobility, enabled by AI.

"There are opportunities in healthcare where AI is bridging the gap to solve for unmet needs—and that’s exactly what Erica and her team are doing at MedHaul,” James Norman, co-founder & managing partner at Black Ops VC, said in a statement. The user-friendly interface and focus on high-acuity communities “make MedHaul an attractive investment, and we’re proud to help them scale.”

Plybeah, who grew up in the Deep South with a grandmother who had diabetic and a double-leg amputee, saw how transportation issues affected her access to care. Throughout her career in clinical and research informatics, including at Epic Systems and St. Jude Children’s Research Hospital, Plybeah “saw how transportation impacted institutions, when patients don’t show up on time, don’t show up at all, show up at the wrong facility … and it brought me back to being a little girl in the Mississippi Delta.”

Over half of MedHaul’s rides require mobility assistance, and 42% live in rural communities. MedHaul’s riders averaged eight rides per year in 2025.

Patients typically don’t pay anything for the rides booked on MedHaul. Instead, the MedHaul client—a provider or payer, for instance—sponsors the rides, often from an existing medical transport budget. In specialties with high transport volumes, providers may also use a patient’s benefit to find rides.

Same-day rides are available within 30 minutes to an hour, depending on acuity. For higher-acuity patients, a booking can take two to three hours, depending on the complexity of the transport required. MedHaul sees a 97% ride completion rate. Providers using MedHaul have reduced missed appointments by 25% and improved patient appointment timeliness by 52%, the company claims.

MedHaul has long used AI across its business. It has a conversational AI agent for older adults who prefer phone calls to texting. It also leverages AI agents to book rides within seconds for highly complex patients, and offers care teams MedHaul GPT, which they can use to ask questions about their programs and patients.

“We really prioritize good implementation,” Plybeah said, noting that there has been an explosion in digital health point solutions, but implementation is a whole different question. “We want this process to be as easy as possible for the end users.”

The company, which launched in 2019, is a public benefit corporation and a certified B Corp. While it’s still a for-profit business, its structure requires it to pursue a stated public benefit alongside financial returns. “We wanted our mission to extend beyond profit and to attract the right partners, investors, employees and customers who shared that commitment,” Plybeah said. “Our social mission is embedded in how we operate, build technology, serve patients and choose the people and organizations we work with.”

Plybeah is excited that there is more industry awareness of the social determinants of health. In the early MedHaul days, “folks seemed to think of transportation as a separate service,” she said. “And now I am grateful to see that folks are really considering transportation as part of the patient’s care journey, because it is.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/texas-ag-drops-allegations-epic-restricts-parents-access-health-data-antitrust-lawsuit" hreflang="en">Texas AG narrows lawsuit against Epic, dropping claims over parental access settings</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Texas AG narrows lawsuit against Epic, dropping claims over parental access settings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Texas is dropping a part of its lawsuit against Epic Systems, specifically the claims about how the company's software handles parental access to a child's electronic health records.

In December, Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton filed a lawsuit against Epic, alleging that the health tech company monopolizes the electronic health record market and cements its dominance in the market through an "anticompetitive playbook."

The lawsuit, filed in Tarrant County District Court, also accused the company of engaging in deceptive practices that restrict parental access to their minor children’s medical records. On Tuesday, the Texas AG's office filed a notice of partial non-suit indicating it was dropping the deceptive practices claims against Epic.

The notice of partial non-suit states that the decision was made "in light of Epic’s recent changes to its software."

In previous statements, an Epic spokesperson said the company "does not determine parental access to children’s medical records."

"Decisions about parental access to children’s medical records are made by doctors and health systems, not by Epic," the spokesperson said.

The state is still moving forward with its antitrust claims under the Texas Free Enterprise and Antitrust Act. The Texas Attorney General's office alleges Epic "uses a wide range of exclusionary tactics to prevent potential competition from its partners, customers and even its own employees." According to the suit, these anticompetitive practices harm Texas hospitals and Texas patients by raising costs and blocking innovative technologies.

In the lawsuit, Paxton alleged that Epic "exploits its control" over patient medical records to automatically hide children’s medication lists, treatment notes and provider messages from parents when their child reaches 12 years old. Texas alleged that Epic’s default proxy-access configurations impede parents’ ability to see their children’s health information.

"These deceptive practices undermine the fundamental right of parents to direct their children’s healthcare" and violate Texas law, which grants parents complete and unrestricted access to their children’s medical records, the attorney general's office said in the lawsuit.

In a statement issued in December, Paxton referred to Epic as a "woke corporation" and stated that Epic's default proxy-access configurations "undermine the sacred rights of parents to protect and oversee their kids’ medical well-being."

In a statement provided to Fierce Healthcare on Wednesday, an Epic spokesperson said the Texas lawsuit includes "significant inaccuracies, including claims about MyChart's customer proxy settings."

"The state filed a voluntary dismissal of those claims on Tuesday. Ken Paxton's office requested that we change our customers' system settings. We said no because those decisions should be made by doctors and health systems and not by Epic. The lawsuit followed. The State should dismiss the remaining claims," the Epic spokesperson said.

In a Jan. 20 filing in Tarrant County District Court, the electronic health record giant said it makes "highly configurable software so that its customers, healthcare organizations across all fifty states, can determine what workflows and functionalities are needed to achieve their own compliance obligations—including with respect to parental access rights to medical records."

"What might be compliant in Texas might not be compliant in another state and vice versa, and those laws may change from time to time, as the law just did roughly four months ago in Texas. Configurability helps customers bridge that gap," the company wrote in its filing.

Epic said it would not "unilaterally go into each of its customers’ systems to change their individualized settings."

Epic also argued, in its court filing, that the state failed to produce facts or legitimate legal bases to support any of its claims. "When Epic asked the State to identify any Epic customers it claimed were not in compliance with the new law, the State did not identify a single Epic customer. What appears to be going on here is that the State has decided to target Epic over a politicized issue regarding proxy access that Epic does not belong in the middle of. The State confirms as much in its press release, calling Epic a 'woke corporation' that supposedly 'undermine[s]' parents’ rights," Epic wrote in its answer to the lawsuit.

In its Answer and Affirmative Defenses, Epic said that the state's antitrust claims against it are pre-textual and baseless. Epic denied Paxton's allegations of anticompetitive behavior, citing a lack of evidence and factual flaws in the lawsuit.

The case will carry over into a changing political landscape in Texas. Paxton, who launched the lawsuit, is leaving the Texas attorney general's office after more than a decade, after his unsuccessful bid for the U.S. Senate. Democrat Nathan Johnson defeated Republican Mayes Middleton in the Nov. 3 midterm election, becoming the first Democrat elected Texas attorney general since 1994.

The leadership change could bring a shift in the office's litigation priorities. Paxton's tenure was marked by high-profile legal battles over social and cultural issues, including transgender healthcare and parental rights.

Last year, the Texas Office of the Attorney General secured a settlement with Austin Diagnostic Clinic that required full restoration of parental proxy access for children aged 12 to 17. Paxton has also issued civil investigative demands to other EHR software providers over parental access to minor children's medical records.

The case will also have a new judge. Democrat Katherine Kim defeated Republican incumbent H. Dustin "Dusty" Fillmore III in the race for Tarrant County's 236th Judicial District Court.

«Diecimila bambini di 31 Paesi in tutto il mondo ringraziano l’Inter che li ha accompagnati nella magnifica avventura di Inter Campus per 30 anni. D’ora in poi continueranno a esprimere la loro felicità con la nuova Fondazione “We Are The Campus” che si avvarrà, oltre che del nostro sostegno, della generosità e della partecipazione di chi vorrà condividere i loro momenti di gioia. Ci dispiace non poter più giocare in nome dell’Inter, che rimarrà nel nostro cuore, così come sulle nostre maglie». Anche il Corriere della Sera pubblica le parole di una lettera che è stata diffusa dalla famiglia Moratti e che annuncia di fatto la seperazione di Inter Campus dal club nerazzurro.

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Il progetto fondato dal presidente e dai suoi figli nel 1997 è stato portato avanti sul campo finora in 31 Paesi al mondo: la maglia dell'Inter è arrivata a regalare gioia e speranza ai bimbi delle zone più in difficoltà. La bandiera nerazzurra ha raggiunto in nome della solidarietà e del calcio i Paesi più dimenticati. E per questo l'ONU ha più volte riconosciuto meriti all'Inter e nel 2022 il club e Inter Campus sono stati inseriti nell'iniziativa delle Nazioni Unite Football for the Goals. E ad aprile 2026, in occasione della Giornata Mondiale dello Sport per lo Sviluppo e la Pace, il progetto nerazzurro è tornato al Palazzo di Vetro delle Nazioni Unite per condividere la propria esperienza pluridecennale nel campo dello sport e dei diritti dell’infanzia.

"La lettera firmata da Carlotta e Massimo Moratti ha il sapore dell’addio tra la società nerazzurra e il campus benefico", scrive il Corriere che riporta anche una precisazione della società nerazzurra: «la collaborazione prosegue tanto è vero che il nome Inter sarà ancora presente».

Il nome Inter sarà ancora presente così come le maglie nerazzurre, ma il progetto - che ora si chiama We are The Campus - andrà avanti grazie ai Moratti e a Tronchetti Provera come ha spiegato nella sua lettera Carlotta, la figlia dell'ex proprietario nerazzurro, a capo del programma solidale.

�Il progetto resta intatto, ma cambia la governance e vive parallelo all’Inter. Non sarà più il club a gestirlo, per sua decisione, ma un nuovo board formato dalla nostra famiglia, da Marco Tronchetti Provera e dal figlio Giovanni. L’importante è mantenere la meraviglia che sta funzionando da così tanto tempo e in così tanti Paesi, puntando a crescere ancora», le sue parole.

Lautaro Martinez scalpita per tornare. Il capitano dell'Inter sta lavorando anche durante la sosta per essere a disposizione di Cristian Chivu già per la gara contro l'Atalanta. Ma come si regolerà l'allenatore nerazzurro considerando anche i problemi degli altri attaccanti? Dalla Gazzetta dello Sport arriva un focus sulle possibili scelte per la prossima di campioanto:

"Bonny non lo ritroverà né in campo né in panchina alla ripresa del campionato. Il risentimento muscolare ai flessori della coscia destra che ha accusato terrà l’ivoriano lontano dal campo per una mesata ma già ha iniziato le terapie. Stesso discorso per Thuram ma l’infortunio del francese è ben diverso rispetto a quello del compagno.

L’affaticamento al momento non preoccupa affatto ma la Francia, di concerto con l’Inter, ha preferito rimandarlo a casa per evitare guai o complicazioni evitabilissime. Ieri Tikus era già ad Appiano, ha iniziato a lavorare a parte e Chivu si augura che a Bergamo ci sia anche lui (strapazzato nell’ultimo periodo senza la metà sudamericana della ThuLa), ma da vedere se dal primo minuto.

Un altro motivo per cui il pieno recupero di Lautaro diventa fondamentale. È presto per pensarci, ma contro l’Atalanta con il Toro e Pio — che comunque molto probabilmente rientrerà alla base con altri 180’ di Nazionale sulle gambe e poco riposato — è pronta a riaccendersi la Pi-La dal primo minuto, come non accade dallo scorso 5 settembre (Inter-Napoli, il 3-2 in rimonta)".

Supabase vs Firebase: Which Backend Fits Your Startup?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 12 • Originally published at tjvex.com

Every startup reaches the same fork in the road. You have an app idea, a small team, and a launch date that will not wait. You need user accounts, a database, file storage, and realtime updates. Building all of that from scratch takes months you do not have. Supabase vs Firebase is the comparison most founders end up making. Switching backends later is the kind of migration nobody budgets for.

This post compares the two platforms on the things that actually decide a startup backend: the database, auth, realtime, pricing, and how hard it is to leave.

Firebase is Google's backend platform, and it has been around long enough to be boring in the best sense. It gives you a NoSQL database called Firestore, authentication, file storage, cloud functions, hosting, push notifications, and analytics, all under one roof. You wire up the SDK, and your app talks to Google's infrastructure directly. There is no server for you to manage.

The biggest strength of Firebase is how fast it gets a product out the door. A solo founder or a two-person team can ship login, a database, and file uploads in days, not weeks. The documentation is extensive, the community is huge, and almost every problem you hit has already been answered somewhere.

Firebase also scales without you thinking about it. Your app goes from ten users to ten thousand, and Firestore keeps answering. That is genuinely valuable when you are small, because your team is building features instead of babysitting infrastructure.

Supabase is the newer challenger, and it takes a different approach. Where Firebase is a set of Google services with its own way of doing things, Supabase is built on open source tools you may already know: PostgreSQL for the database, GoTrue for auth, and standard storage and realtime layers on top. It calls itself the open source Firebase alternative, and that framing is accurate.

The practical difference is that Supabase gives you a real relational database. Your data lives in Postgres tables with rows, columns, foreign keys, and SQL queries. If your team already knows SQL, and most backend developers do, Supabase feels familiar from day one. You can write a complex query, join three tables, and get exactly the rows you need, instead of structuring your whole data model around the limits of a document store.

Supabase is also self-hostable. You can run the whole stack on your own servers if you ever need to, which matters to teams selling into regulated industries, or to clients who ask where their data lives.

The database is the decision you will live with longest, so it deserves the most attention. Firestore is a document database. You store collections of documents, each one a bundle of fields, and you query them with the rules Firestore allows. It is fast for simple lookups, and it handles nesting naturally.

The trouble starts with relationships. A marketplace with sellers, products, orders, and reviews needs joins, and Firestore does not do joins. You end up duplicating data across documents, or making several reads where one query would do. That works, and plenty of successful apps do exactly this, but it pushes complexity into your app code.

Postgres, the database behind Supabase, handles relationships natively. Orders link to users, products link to sellers, and a single query pulls the whole picture together. For products with connected data, like booking systems, marketplaces, or CRMs, that difference shows up fast.

There is a counterpoint worth stating plainly. If your data is simple, like a chat app or a feed, Firestore is easier. You will write less backend code and move faster.

Both platforms handle the part every app needs and nobody enjoys building: sign-up, login, password resets, and social login with Google or Apple. Firebase Auth is polished and battle tested, with phone auth and anonymous sign-in built in. Supabase Auth covers the same ground, including social providers, magic links, and phone auth.

One practical difference is how user data connects to the rest of your app. In Supabase, your users table is a normal Postgres table, so linking a user to their orders or subscription is one foreign key. In Firebase, user records live in Auth and app data lives in Firestore, so you connect the two by user ID and keep them in sync yourself.

Both platforms offer realtime updates, which is how a chat message or a booking change appears on screen without a refresh. Firebase built its reputation on realtime, and it shows. Firestore listeners are simple to use and reliable. Supabase realtime is built on Postgres replication, which broadcasts database changes to connected clients. It works well for most cases, but Firebase still has the edge in maturity here.

File storage is similar on both. You upload images or documents to a bucket, set access rules, and serve them through a CDN. Supabase storage is S3-compatible, which makes it easy to move files elsewhere later.

Both platforms offer serverless functions for the logic that should not live in the app: sending an email after an order, charging a card, or cleaning up old data. For a startup, the honest summary is that both are fine. The functions you write in the first year will be small, and either platform runs them well.

This is where founders get caught out, so read the pricing pages carefully before you commit. Firebase charges by usage: reads, writes, deletes, storage, and bandwidth. The free tier is generous, and for a small app the bill can stay at zero for months. The surprise comes with scale. A chatty app that reads a document every time a user opens a screen can rack up millions of reads, and each one is billed.

Supabase charges by project tier, with a free tier and paid plans that include a bundle of database size, storage, and bandwidth. The pricing is easier to predict, because you are paying for a plan rather than counting individual reads. Heavy read patterns that would sting on Firebase are often cheaper on Supabase, since a SQL query that returns a hundred rows is one query, not a hundred reads.

Every backend platform locks you in to some degree. The question is how much, and what leaving costs. Firebase lock-in is real. Your data lives in Firestore, your auth lives in Google, your functions run on Google Cloud. Moving away means rewriting data access code and migrating users, which is a project, not an afternoon.

Supabase lock-in is lighter, because the core is Postgres. Your data is in a standard database you can export with ordinary tools, and you can even self-host the whole stack. That does not make migration free, since your app code still talks to Supabase libraries, but the data layer, which is the expensive part to move, is portable by design.

There is no universal winner, but there are clear patterns. Pick Firebase if you are a small team shipping a mobile app fast, your data is simple, and you are comfortable inside Google's ecosystem. It remains an excellent choice for MVPs and consumer apps where speed to launch beats everything else.

Pick Supabase if your data is relational, your team knows SQL, you expect complex queries and reporting, or your clients care about data portability. It is also the stronger choice when your product is a dashboard, a marketplace, a booking system, or a CRM.

In our experience, teams that choose Firebase for speed and later regret it usually regret the document model, not the platform. Teams that choose Supabase and regret it usually wanted a faster start. Knowing which regret you are more likely to have is half the decision.

Yes, but treat it as a real project. You will migrate data, move user accounts, rewrite queries, and retest realtime features. It is doable, and teams do it, but it typically takes weeks, not days.

Yes. It powers production apps with real traffic. Like any young platform, check that the specific features you need are stable before you commit.

It depends on your usage pattern. Read-heavy apps are often cheaper on Supabase, because one SQL query can replace many document reads. Small apps with light usage can run free on either.

Yes. The official Supabase Flutter package covers auth, database, realtime, and storage. If your team builds in Flutter, both platforms have solid support, so let the data model decide rather than the SDK.

Write down your data model before you pick a platform. List your entities, how they relate, and the five hardest queries your app will run. If those queries are simple lookups, Firebase will serve you well. If they are joins, filters, and reports, Supabase will save you pain later.

If you would like help making the call, book a free strategy call with us (https://tjvex.com/contact/). We will look at your data model and your launch timeline, and tell you honestly which backend fits. We aim to reply within 24 hours.
